 
 
 
 [math/0312479] Global existence for the Einstein vacuum equationsin wave coordinates 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

 
 


 
 
 
 
 
 
 Global existence for the Einstein vacuum equations
 in wave coordinates 
 
 
 Hans Lindblad   and Igor Rodnianski
 
 Thanks:  Part of this work was done while H.L. was a Member of the
Institute for Advanced Study, Princeton, supported by the NSF grant
DMS-0111298 to the Institute. H.L. was also partially supported
by the NSF Grant DMS-0200226. 
 
 Thanks:  Part of this work was done while I.R. was a
Clay Mathematics Institute Long-Term
Prize Fellow. His work was also partially supported by the NSF grant DMS–01007791.
 
 Affiliation:  University of California at San Diego and Princeton University
 

 Abstract 
 
 We prove global stability of Minkowski space for the Einstein vacuum
equations in harmonic (wave) coordinate gauge for the set of
restricted data coinciding with the Schwarzschild solution in the
neighborhood of space-like infinity. The result contradicts previous
beliefs that wave coordinates are ”unstable in the large” and
provides an alternative approach to the stability problem originally
solved ( for unrestricted data, in a different gauge and with a
precise description of the asymptotic behavior at null infinity) by
D. Christodoulou and S. Klainerman. 
 
 Using the
wave coordinate gauge we recast the Einstein equations as a system
of quasilinear wave equations and, in absence of the classical null
condition, establish a small data global existence result. In our
previous work we introduced the notion of a weak null condition 
and showed that the
Einstein equations in harmonic coordinates satisfy this condition.The
result of this paper relies on this observation and combines it with
the vector field method based on the symmetries of the standard
Minkowski space. 
 
 In a forthcoming paper we will address the question of stability of Minkowski
space for the Einstein vacuum equations in wave coordinates for all ”small”
asymptotically flat data and the case of the Einstein equations coupled to a scalar
field. 
 
 
 
 1 Introduction 

 
 The focus of this paper is the question of global existence and
stability for the Einstein vacuum equations in ”harmonic” (wave
coordinate) gauge. The Einstein equations determine a 4-d manifold
 ℳ {\cal M} with a Lorentzian metric g g with vanishing Ricci
curvature 
 

 
 
 R μ ​ ν = 0 . R_{\mu\nu}=0. 
 
 
 We consider the initial value problem: for a given a 3-d manifold Σ \Sigma ,
with a Riemannian metric g 0 g_{0} , and a symmetric two-tensor k 0 k_{0} , we
want to find a 4-d manifold ℳ {\cal M} , with a Lorentzian metric
 g g satisfying the Einstein equations, and an imbedding
 Σ ⊂ M \Sigma\subset M such that g 0 g_{0} is the restriction of g g to
 Σ \Sigma and k 0 k_{0} is the second fundamental form of Σ \Sigma in
 ℳ {\cal M} . The initial value problem is overdetermined which
imposes compatibility conditions on the initial data: the
constraint equations 
 

 
 
 R 0 − k 0 j i k 0 j i + k 0 i i k 0 j j = 0 , ∇ j k 0 i ​ j − ∇ i k 0 j j = 0 , ∀ i = 1 , … , 3 . R_{0}-{k_{0}}_{j}^{i}\,{k_{0}}^{j}_{i}+{k_{0}}_{i}^{i}\,{k_{0}}_{j}^{j}=0,\qquad\nabla^{j}{k_{0}}_{ij}-\nabla_{i}\,{k_{0}}_{j}^{j}=0,\qquad\forall i=1,...,3. 
 
 
 Here R 0 R_{0} is the scalar curvature of g 0 g_{0} and ∇ \nabla is
covariant differentiation with respect to g 0 g_{0} . The Einstein
equations are invariant under diffeomorphisms. To have a working
formulation one needs to eliminate this freedom by fixing a gauge
condition or a system of coordinates. 
 
 
 While the Einstein equations are
independent of the choice of a coordinate system, the existence of
a special or preferred system of coordinates has been a subject of
debate [ Fo ] . Historically, the first special coordinates were
the harmonic coordinates (also referred to as wave 
coordinates in current terminology). These obey the equation
 □ g ​ x μ = 0 \square_{g}\,x^{\mu}=0 , μ = 0 , 1 , 2 , 3 \mu=0,1,2,3 , where □ g = ∇ α ∇ α \square_{g}=\nabla_{\alpha}\nabla^{\alpha} is the geometric wave operator. Relative to the wave
coordinates a Lorentzian metric g g satisfies the wave 
coordinate condition if: 1 1 
 1 
 
 
 We shall use below the standard
convention of summing over repeated indices and the notation
 ∂ α = ∂ / ∂ x α \partial_{\alpha}=\partial/\partial x^{\alpha} 
 

 
 (1.1) 
 
 g α ​ β ∂ β g α ​ μ = 1 2 g α ​ β ∂ μ g α ​ β , ∀ μ = 0 , . . , 3 . g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}g_{\alpha\mu}=\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha\beta},\qquad\forall\mu=0,..,3. 
 
 
 In this system of coordinates, the vacuum Einstein equations
take the form of a system of quasilinear wave equations 
 

 
 (1.2) 
 
 g α ​ β ​ ∂ α ∂ β g μ ​ ν = 𝒩 μ ​ ν ​ ( g , ∂ g ) , ∀ μ , ν = 0 , . . , 3 g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\,g_{\mu\nu}={\cal N}_{\mu\nu}(g,\partial g),\qquad\forall\mu,\nu=0,..,3 
 
 
 with a nonlinearity 𝒩 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\cal N}(u,v) depending quadratically on v v .
In this particular gauge Choquet-Bruhat [ CB1 ] was able to
establish the existence of a globally hyperbolic
development 2 2 
 2 
 
 
 For the definitions of global hyperbolicity and
maximal Cauchy development see [ H-E ] , [ Wa ] of the
Einstein vacuum equations starting with an arbitrary set of initial
data prescribed on a 3-d space-like hypersurface and satisfying the
constraint equations. While the result of Choquet-Bruhat and a later
result of Choquet-Bruhat and Geroch [ CB-G ] , establishing the
existence of a maximal Cauchy development, constructs solutions for
any given initial data set, it does not provide any information
about the geodesic completeness of the obtained solution. In the
language of the evolution equations these results only show the
existence of ”local in time” solutions. 
 
 
 The global results have proved to be by far more resistant. The
outstanding global problem, which for a long time remained open, and
was finally ingeniously solved by Christodoulou and Klainerman
 [ C-K ] , was that of the stability of Minkowski space. In
simplified language, it is the problem of constructing a global
solution to the Einstein vacuum equations from the initial data,
which is close to the Minkowski metric m μ ​ ν m_{\mu\nu} , and
asymptotically approaching the Minkowski space. The initial data
 ( Σ , g 0 , k 0 ) (\Sigma,g_{0},k_{0}) for the problem of stability of Minkowski space is
asymptotically flat, i.e., the complement of a compact set in Σ \Sigma 
is diffeomorphic to the complement of a ball in ℝ 3 {\mathbb{R}}^{3} , and
there exists a system of coordinates ( x 1 , x 2 , x 3 ) (x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}) with
 r = x 1 2 + x 2 2 + x 3 2 r=\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}} such that for all sufficiently large
 r r the metric 3 3 
 3 
 
 
 The stability result of [ C-K ] was
proved for strongly asymptotically flat data g 0 i ​ j = ( 1 + 2 M / r ) δ i ​ j + o ( r − 3 / 2 ) {g_{0}}_{ij}=(1+2M/r)\delta_{ij}+o(r^{-3/2}) ,
 k 0 = o ( r − 5 / 2 ) k_{0}=o(r^{-5/2}) . 
 g 0 i ​ j = ( 1 + 2 ​ M / r ) ​ δ i ​ j + o ⁡ ( r − 1 − σ ) {g_{0}}_{ij}=(1+2M/r)\delta_{ij}+o(r^{-1-\sigma}) , and the second
fundamental form k 0 = o ⁡ ( r − 2 − σ ) k_{0}=o(r^{-2-\sigma}) for some σ > 0 \sigma>0 . Here M M is
the mass, which by the positive mass theorem is positive unless the
data is flat, see Schoen and Yau [ S-Y ] and Witten [ Wi ] . In
addition, the data is required to satisfy a global smallness
assumption, which makes sure that it is sufficiently close to the
data ( ℝ 3 , δ , 0 ) ({\mathbb{R}}^{3},\delta,0) for the Minkowski space. 
 
 
 To understand some of the difficulties of the problem we recall
that a generic system of quasilinear equations 
 

 
 (1.3) 
 
 □ ​ ϕ I = ∑ | α | ≤ | β | ≤ 2 A I , α ​ β J ​ K ​ ∂ α ϕ J ​ ∂ β ϕ K + cubic terms \square\phi_{I}=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq|\beta|\leq 2}A_{I,\alpha\beta}^{JK}\partial^{\alpha}\phi_{J}\partial^{\beta}\phi_{K}+{\text{cubic terms}} 
 
 
 allows solutions with smooth arbitrarily small initial data which
blow up in finite time 4 4 
 4 
 
 
 This is in particular true for a
 semilinear equation □ ​ ϕ = ( ∂ t ϕ ) 2 \square\phi=(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2} ,
 [ J1 ] . . The key to global existence for such equations was
the null condition found by Klainerman,
 [ K2 ] .
The small data global existence result for the equations satisfying the
null condition was established in [ C1 ] , [ K2 ] .
The null condition manifests itself in special
algebraic cancellations in the coefficients A I , α ​ β J ​ K A_{I,\alpha\beta}^{JK} of
the quadratic terms of the equation. 5 5 
 5 
 
 
 E.g. □ ​ ϕ = ( ∂ t ϕ ) 2 − | ∇ x ϕ | 2 \square\phi=(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}-|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2} satisfies the
null-condition. It can be shown however, that the Einstein vacuum
equations in wave coordinates do not satisfy the null condition.
Moreover, Choquet-Bruhat [ CB3 ] showed that even without
imposing a specific gauge the Einstein equations violate the null
condition. 
 
 
 These considerations led to the suggestion that the wave coordinates
are not suitable for proving stability of Minkowski space. In fact,
considering a second iterate of the equation ( 1.2 ),
Choquet-Bruhat [ CB2 ] argued that the Einstein vacuum equations
are not stable in wave coordinates near the Minkowski solution. All
these resulted in the belief that the wave coordinates are unstable
in the large in the sense that a possible finite time blow up of
solutions of the equation ( 1.2 ) is due to a coordinate
singularity. 
 
 
 The global stability of Minkowski space had been proved by
Christodoulou and Klainerman [ C-K ] who avoided the use of a
preferred system of coordinates and instead relied on the
invariant formulation of the Einstein equations with the choice of
maximal time foliation (or the double null foliation in the new
proof of Klainerman and Nicolo [ K-N1 ] ) and utilizing Bianchi
identities for the curvature. The special structure of the
quadratic terms plays a crucial part in the generalized energy
estimates which form the backbone of the proof but the null
condition can not be pointed out precisely. 
 
 
 A semiglobal stability result was also obtained in the work of
Friedrich [ Fr ] . He used the conformal method
to reduce the global problem to a local one. The approach is
invariant and the special structure is again exploited implicitly. 
 
 
 In this paper we revisit the problem of global stability of Minkowski
space in wave coordinates. More precisely, we consider the data 6 6 
 6 
 
 
 The
existence of such data is guaranteed by the results of Corvino and Chrusciel-Delay,
 [ Co ] , [ C-D ] . 
 ( ℝ 3 , g 0 , k 0 ) ({\mathbb{R}}^{3},g_{0},k_{0}) with the metric g 0 g_{0} coinciding with the
spatial part of the Schwarzschild metric
 g S = ( 1 + M / r ) 4 ​ d ​ x 2 g_{S}=(1+M/r)^{4}dx^{2} 
in the region r > 1 >> M r>1>>M , vanishing second fundamental form k 0 k_{0} for r > 1 r>1 ,
and satisfying a global smallness assumption on ℝ 3 {\mathbb{R}}^{3} .
We prove that for this initial data the wave coordinate gauge is stable in
the large: the reduced Einstein equations ( 1.2 ) has a global
solution g g defining a future causally geodesically complete space-time,
 [ H-E ] .
The metric g g in wave coordinates x α , α = 0 , . . , 3 x^{\alpha},\,\alpha=0,..,3 
approaches the Minkowski metric m m :  
 sup x ∈ ℝ 3 | g ⁡ ( t , x ) − m | → 0 \sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}|g(t,x)-m|\to 0 as t → ∞ t\to\infty . 
 
 
 The intuition behind this result is based on the observation that
the Einstein vacuum equations in wave coordinates ( 1.2 )
satisfy the weak null condition . This notion was introduced
in [ L-R ] for general quasilinear systems ( 1.3 ) and
requires that the corresponding effective asymptotic system 
 

 
 (1.4) 
 
 ( ∂ t + ∂ r ) ( ∂ t − ∂ r ) Φ I = r − 1 ∑ n ≤ m ≤ 2 A I , n ​ m J ​ K ( ∂ t − ∂ r ) n Φ J ( ∂ t − ∂ r ) m Φ K , Φ I ∼ r ϕ I (\partial_{t}+\partial_{r})(\partial_{t}-\partial_{r})\Phi_{I}={r}^{-1}\sum_{n\leq m\leq 2}A_{I,nm}^{JK}(\partial_{t}-\partial_{r})^{n}\Phi_{J}\,\,(\partial_{t}-\partial_{r})^{m}\Phi_{K},\qquad\Phi_{I}\sim r\phi_{I} 
 
 
 has global solutions for all small initial data. 7 7 
 7 
 
 
 For the
precise definition see section 6 . Here, 
 

 
 
 A I , n ​ m J ​ K ​ ( ω ) = ∑ | α | = n , | β | = m A I , α ​ β J ​ K ​ ω ^ α ​ ω ^ β , ω ^ = ( − 1 , ω ) , ω ∈ 𝕊 2 . A_{I,nm}^{JK}(\omega)=\sum_{|\alpha|=n,|\beta|=m}A_{I,\alpha\beta}^{JK}\hat{\omega}^{\alpha}\hat{\omega}^{\beta},\qquad\hat{\omega}=(-1,\omega),\,\,\omega\in{\mathbb{S}}^{2}. 
 
 
 The classical null condition states that A I , n ​ m J ​ K ​ ( ω ) ≡ 0 A_{I,nm}^{JK}(\omega)\equiv 0 and thus implies the weak null condition. The asymptotic
system ( 1.4 ) arises as an approximation of
( 1.3 ) when one neglects the derivatives tangential to the
outgoing Minkowski light cones, known to have faster decay.
The asymptotic equation was introduced in [ H1 ] to
predict the time of a blow-up for scalar wave equations known to
blow up in finite time, and was used in [ L2 ] to find some other scalar
wave equations for which the known blow-up mechanism was not
present. Asymptotic systems played an important role in the
analysis of the blow-up mechanisms in [ A1 ] . 
 
 
 In [ L-R ] we have shown that the asymptotic system generated by the
Einstein equations in wave coordinates ( 1.2 ) has global solutions
for all data. In this paper we consider the full nonlinear system
( 1.2 ).
We should note that although the asymptotic system provides
useful heuristics about the behavior of solutions, in particular the
 L ∞ L^{\infty} decay of the first derivatives of various components of
the metric g g , it is barely used in our proof of the small data global existence
result for the full nonlinear equation ( 1.2 ). While it is tempting to
put forward a conjecture that, parallel to the result for the classical null
condition [ C1 ] , [ K2 ] , the weak null condition guarantees the
global existence result for small initial data, we can only argue that all
known examples seem to confirm it.
A simple example of an equation satisfying the weak null condition, violating
the standard null condition and yet possessing global solutions for all data
is given by the system 
 

 
 (1.5) 
 
 □ ​ ϕ = w ⋅ ∂ 2 ϕ + ∂ ψ ⋅ ∂ ψ , □ ​ ψ = 0 , □ ​ w = 0 \square\phi=w\cdot\partial^{2}\phi+\partial\psi\cdot\partial\psi,\qquad\quad\square\psi=0,\qquad\square w=0 
 
 
 Another example is provided by the equation □ ​ ϕ = ϕ ​ Δ ​ ϕ \square\phi=\phi\Delta\phi . The proof of a small data global existence result
for this equation is quite involved, [ L2 ] (radial case),
 [ A3 ] . As we show in this paper the Einstein equations
( 1.2 ) is yet another example. Interestingly enough, at
the level of an effective asymptotic system the Einstein equations
can be modelled by the system ( 1.5 ). 
 
 
 The asymptotic behavior of null components
of the Riemann curvature tensor R α ​ β ​ γ ​ δ R_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta} of metric g g - the so called
”peeling estimates”- was discussed in the works of Bondi, Sachs and Penrose
and becomes important in the framework of asymptotically
simple space-times (roughly speaking, space-times which can be conformally
compactified), see also the paper of Christodoulou [ C2 ] for further discussion
of such space-times. Global solutions obtained in the work [ C-K ] were
accompanied by very precise analysis of its asymptotic behavior although not
entirely consistent with peeling estimates. However, global solutions obtained
by Klainerman-Nicolo [ K-N1 ] in the exterior 8 8 
 8 
 
 
 Outside of the domain
of dependence of a compact set stability of Minkowski space
were shown to possess peeling estimates for special initial data, [ K-N2 ] . 
 
 
 Our work is less precise about the asymptotic behavior and is focused more on
developing a technically relatively simple approach allowing us to prove stability
of Minkowski space in a physically interesting wave coordinate gauge
In particular, we rely only on the standard Killing and conformal
Killing vector fields of Minkowski space and do not construct almost
Killing and conformal Killing vector fields adapted to the geometry
of null cones of the solution g g . 
 
 
 Our proof is based on generalized energy estimates combined with
decay estimates. The generalized energy estimates are used with
Minkowski vector fields { ∂ α , Ω α ​ β = x α ∂ β − x β ∂ α , S = x α ∂ α } \{\partial_{\alpha},\Omega_{\alpha\beta}=x_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}-x_{\beta}\partial_{\alpha},S=x^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\} . For the equations satisfying the standard
null condition uniform in time bounds on the generalized energies,
combined with global Sobolev (Klainerman-Sobolev) inequalities, are sufficient to infer
small data global existence. In our case however the generalized
energies slowly grow in time (at the rate of t ε t^{\varepsilon} ) and need to
be complemented by independent, not following from the global
Sobolev inequalities, decay estimates. We derive the latter by
direct integration of the equation along the characteristics. It
is at this point that the intuition from the effective asymptotic
system is most useful. We show that all components of the metric
with exception of one decay at the rate of t − 1 t^{-1} . The remaining
component however decays only as t − 1 + ε t^{-1+\varepsilon} . Somewhat
surprisingly, the glue that holds together such weak decay
estimates and the generalized energy estimates is the wave coordinate
condition ( 1.1 ). 
 
 
 In this paper we only prove the result for a restricted set of data
coinciding with the Schwarzschild data outside of the ball of radius
one. 9 9 
 9 
 
 
 Since the initial metric is always of the form
 g i ​ j = ( 1 + 4 ​ M / r ) ​ δ i ​ j + o ⁡ ( r − 1 ) g_{ij}=(1+4M/r)\delta_{ij}+o(r^{-1}) with M > 0 M>0 , data coinciding
with the Schwarzschild outside of a compact set is the closest analogue
of compactly supported or rapidly
decaying data usually considered in small data global existence results
for nonlinear wave equations. This allows us to somewhat sidestep the problem of a
long range effect of a gravitational field. Due to the inward
bending of the light rays, solution arising from initial data
coinciding with the Schwarzschild data outside of the ball of radius
one will be equal to the Schwarzschild solution in the exterior of
the Minkowski cone r = t + 1 r=t+1 . 
 
 
 In our subsequent work we hope to be able to prove the stability of Minkowski
space in wave coordinates for general data.
In addition we hope to show that our method can be also used to treat the problem
of small data global existence for the Einstein
equations coupled to a scalar field.

 2 The main results and the strategy of the proof 

 
 We now formulate the main results of our paper.
Our first result is global existence for the Einstein vacuum
equations in wave coordinates.

 Remark 2.2 . 
 
 The existence of data satisfying the assumptions of the theorem follows from
the work of [ Co ] , [ C-D ] , as argued in section 4. 
 
 
 
 A corollary of the above result is the global stability of Minkowski space for
a restricted set of initial data. 
 
 
 Theorem 2.3 . 
 
 Let ( ℝ 3 , g 0 , k 0 ) ({\mathbb{R}}^{3},g_{0},k_{0}) be the initial data set for the Einstein vacuum
equations R μ ​ ν = 0 R_{\mu\nu}=0 . Assume that relative to some system of coordinates
 ( x 1 , x 2 , x 3 ) (x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}) 
the metric g 0 g_{0} coincides with the spatial part of the
Schwarzschild metric g S g_{S} outside the ball of radius one, 
 

 
 
 g 0 = ( 1 + M r ) 4 ​ d ​ x 2 , r > 1 , g_{0}=(1+\frac{M}{r})^{4}dx^{2},\qquad r>1, 
 
 
 while the second fundamental form k 0 k_{0} vanishes for r > 1 r>1 .
In addition, we assume that relative to that system of coordinates
 g 0 g_{0} , M M and k 0 k_{0} satisfy the smallness condition 
 

 
 
 ∑ 0 ≤ | I | ≤ N ‖ ∂ x I ( g 0 − δ ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( B 1 ) + ∑ 0 ≤ | I | ≤ N − 1 ‖ ∂ x I k 0 ‖ L 2 ​ ( B 1 ) + M < ϵ . \sum_{0\leq|I|\leq N}\|\partial_{x}^{I}(g_{0}-\delta)\|_{L^{2}(B_{1})}+\sum_{0\leq|I|\leq N-1}\|\partial_{x}^{I}k_{0}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1})}+M<\epsilon. 
 
 
 Then there exists a future causally geodesically
complete 11 11 
 11 
 
 
 For the definition see [ H-E ] and section 16
of this paper. solution g g together with a global system of wave
coordinates with the property that the curvature tensor of g g 
relative to these coordinates decays to zero along any future
directed causal geodesic. 
 
 
 
 We now outline the strategy of
the proof. 
 
 
 Remark 2.4 . 
 
 Throughout the paper we shall use the notation
 A ≲ B A\lesssim B for the inequality A ≤ C ​ B A\leq CB with some large
 universal constant C C . In our estimates we will make no
distinction between the tensors h α ​ β = g α ​ β − m α ​ β h_{\alpha\beta}=g_{\alpha\beta}-m_{\alpha\beta} 
and H α ​ β = m α ​ α ′ ​ m β ​ β ′ ​ ( g α ​ β − m α ​ β ) H_{\alpha\beta}=m_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}m_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}(g^{\alpha\beta}-m^{\alpha\beta}) ,
since H = − h + O ⁡ ( h 2 ) H=-h+O(h^{2}) and the terms quadratic in h h are lower order. 
 
 
 
 The continuity argument 
For the proof we let δ \delta be any fixed number
 0 < δ < 1 / 2 0<\delta<1/2 . Let g g be a local smooth solution of the reduced
Einstein equations ( 2.1 ).
We start with the weak estimate 
 

 
 (2.6) 
 
 E N ​ ( t ) ≤ 64 ​ ε 2 ​ ( 1 + t ) 2 ​ δ E_{N}(t)\leq 64\varepsilon^{2}(1+t)^{2\delta} 
 
 
 By assumptions of the Theorem the estimate ( 2.6 )
holds for t = 0 t=0 . Let [ 0 , T ] [0,T] be
the largest time interval on which ( 2.6 ) still holds.
We shall show that if ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 is sufficiently
small then on the interval [ 0 , T ] [0,T] the inequality
( 2.6 ) implies the same inequality with the
constant 64 64 replaced by 16 16 . It will then follow that the
solution and the energy estimate ( 2.6 ) can be
extended to a larger time interval [ 0 , T ′ ] [0,T^{\prime}] thus contradicting the
maximality of T T . This will imply that T = ∞ T=\infty and the
solution is global. We will in fact prove that for a sufficiently
small ε \varepsilon the stronger estimate ( 2.5 )
holds true on the interval [ 0 , T ] [0,T] . 
 
 
 The global Sobolev inequality of Proposition
 9.2 and the
weak energy estimate ( 2.6 ) imply the pointwise
decay estimates: 
 

 
 (2.7) 
 
 ∑ | I | ≤ N − 2 | ∂ Z I ​ h ​ ( t , x ) | ≤ C ​ ε ​ ( 1 + t ) δ ( 1 + t + r ) ​ ( 1 + | t − r | ) 1 / 2 , r = | x | \sum_{|I|\leq N-2}|\partial Z^{I}h(t,x)|\leq\frac{C\varepsilon(1+t)^{\delta}}{(1+t+r)(1+|t-r|)^{1/2}},\qquad r=|x| 
 
 
 From the assumption that the constant δ < 1 / 2 \delta<1/2 
we derive the following weak decay estimates 
 

 
 (2.8) 
 
 | ∂ Z I h ( t , x ) | ≤ C ε ( 1 + t + r ) − 1 / 2 − γ ( 1 + | t − r | ) − 1 / 2 − γ , | I | ≤ N − 2 |\partial Z^{I}h(t,x)|\leq C\varepsilon(1+t+r)^{-1/2-\gamma}(1+|t-r|)^{-1/2-\gamma},\qquad|I|\leq N-2 
 
 
 with some fixed constant γ > 0 \gamma>0 .
The weak decay estimates ( 2.8 )
will lead to
much stronger decay estimates in Theorem
 14.1 .
In turn, using the stronger decay estimates in Theorem
 14.1 we will be able to obtain
stronger energy estimates in Theorem
 15.1 , i.e. ( 2.5 ).
These in particular will enable us to show that the
estimate ( 2.6 ) holds globally in time and conclude the proof.
We remark that in the course of the proof all constants
will be independent of ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 but they will depend
on a lower bound for γ > 0 \gamma>0 (and hence on an upper bound for δ < 1 / 2 \delta<1/2 ). 
 
 
 As described above, the proof is a direct consequence of three
results. First is the global Sobolev inequality of
Proposition 9.2 , introduced by S. Klainerman [ K1 ] , giving decay
estimates in terms of energy estimates for the
generators of the Lorentz group. The second ingredient is the improved
decay estimates in Theorem 14.1 . The final component is
the energy
estimates in Theorem 15.1 which rely on the improved decay
estimates. 
 
 
 Weak decay estimates. 
As pointed out above we may start by
assuming the weak decay estimate ( 2.8 ).
Furthermore, since the solution g = m + h g=m+h coincides with the Schwarzschild
solution of mass M ≤ ε M\leq\varepsilon in the region r ≥ t + 1 r\geq t+1 , we have 
 

 
 (2.9) 
 
 | Z I ​ h ​ ( t , x ) | ≲ ε ​ ( 1 + r + t ) − 1 , when | x | = t + 1 |Z^{I}h(t,x)|\lesssim\varepsilon(1+r+t)^{-1},\qquad\text{when}\quad|x|=t+1 
 
 
 Hence
integrating ( 2.8 ) from the light cone, where
( 2.9 ) holds, we get 
 

 
 (2.10) 
 
 | Z I h ( t , x ) | ≲ ε ( 1 + r + t ) − 1 / 2 − γ ( 1 + | t − r | ) 1 / 2 − γ , |Z^{I}h(t,x)|\lesssim\varepsilon(1+r+t)^{-1/2-\gamma}(1+|t-r|)^{1/2-\gamma}, 
 
 
 Since the vector fields span the tangent space of the outgoing
light cones r − t = q r-t=q we infer, with ∂ ¯ \bar{\partial} denoting the derivatives
tangential to the cones, that 
 

 
 (2.11) 
 
 | ∂ ¯ Z I h | ≲ ε ( 1 + r + t ) − 3 / 2 − γ ( 1 + | t − r | ) 1 / 2 − γ , |\bar{\partial}Z^{I}h|\lesssim\varepsilon(1+r+t)^{-3/2-\gamma}(1+|t-r|)^{1/2-\gamma}, 
 
 
 This means that, close to the light cone t = r t=r , derivatives tangential to the forward light cones decay
quite a bit better than the expected decay rate from ( 2.8 ) for a
generic derivative. 
 
 
 Wave coordinate condition. 
As we shall see below certain
components of the tensor h h decay faster than
others. This can be seen upon introduction of a null frame of vector fields
 L = ∂ t + ∂ r L=\partial_{t}+\partial_{r} , L ¯ = ∂ r − ∂ t \underline{L}=\partial_{r}-\partial_{t} and S 1 , S 2 S_{1},S_{2} : two orthonormal vectors
tangential to the sphere of radius r r in ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} . The first improved
estimates come from the wave coordinate condition
( 2.2 ). Writing
 g α ​ β = m α ​ β + h α ​ β g_{\alpha\beta}=m_{\alpha\beta}+h_{\alpha\beta} we obtain
from ( 2.2 ) that 
 

 
 
 m α ​ β ​ ∂ α h β ​ μ = ∂ μ m α ​ β ​ h α ​ β + O ⁡ ( h ​ ∂ h ) m^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\beta\mu}=\partial_{\mu}m^{\alpha\beta}h_{\alpha\beta}+O(h\,\partial h) 
 
 
 In particular, contracting with a vector field T ∈ 𝒯 = { L , S 1 , S 2 } T\in{\cal T}=\{L,S_{1},S_{2}\} and
using that for any symmetric 2-tensor k k ,
 m α ​ β ​ k α ​ β = − k L ​ L ¯ + δ A ​ B ​ k A ​ B m^{\alpha\beta}k_{\alpha\beta}=-k_{L\underline{L}}+\delta^{AB}k_{AB} , implies that we can express the
transversal derivative ∂ L ¯ \partial_{\underline{L}} of certain components of h h 
in terms of the tangential derivatives that decay better and a
quadratic term 
 

 
 
 | ( ∂ h ) L ​ T | ≤ | ∂ ¯ ​ h | + | h | ​ | ∂ h | ≲ ε ​ ( 1 + t + r ) − 1 − 2 ​ γ , | h L ​ T | ≲ ε ⁡ ( 1 + | t − r | ) ​ ( 1 + t + r ) − 1 |(\partial h)_{LT}|\leq|\bar{\partial}h|+|h|\,|\partial h|\lesssim\varepsilon(1+t+r)^{-1-2\gamma},\qquad|h_{LT}|\lesssim\varepsilon(1+|t-r|)(1+t+r)^{-1} 
 
 
 Even though the estimate above does not give a better decay rate for all
components of h h it gives the decay exactly for those components which, as
it turns out, control the geometry, i.e., they lead to stronger energy and decay
estimates. 
 
 
 The above estimates will be sufficient to obtain improved estimates for the lowest
order energy of h h . However, in order to get estimates for the energy of
 Z I ​ h Z^{I}h we commute the vector fields Z Z through
the equation for h h . This generates additional commutator terms.
The main commutator terms are controlled with the help of the
following additional estimate from the wave coordinate condition: 
 

 
 (2.12) 
 
 | ( ∂ h ) L ​ T | + | ( ∂ Z ​ h ) L ​ L | ≤ ε ​ ( 1 + t + r ) − 1 − 2 ​ γ , | h L ​ T | + | ( Z ​ h ) L ​ L | ≤ ε ⁡ ( 1 + | t − r | ) ​ ( 1 + t + r ) − 1 |(\partial h)_{LT}|+|(\partial Zh)_{LL}|\leq\varepsilon(1+t+r)^{-1-2\gamma},\qquad|h_{LT}|+|(Zh)_{LL}|\leq\varepsilon(1+|t-r|)(1+t+r)^{-1} 
 
 
 We now describe derivation of the stronger decay and energy estimates. 
 
 
 Stronger decay estimates. 
We rely on the following decay
estimate for the wave equation on a curved background
 12 12 
 12 
 
 
 Recall that the reduced wave operator □ ~ g = g α ​ β ∂ 2 α ​ β \widetilde{\square}_{g}=g^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta} . : 
 

 
 (2.13) 
 
 ‖ ( 1 + t + r ) ​ ∂ ϕ ⁡ ( t , ⋅ ) ‖ L ∞ ≤ C ​ ∫ 0 t ( 1 + τ ) ​ ‖ □ ~ g ​ ϕ ​ ( τ , ⋅ ) ‖ L ∞ ​ 𝑑 τ + C sup 0 ≤ τ ≤ t ∑ | I | ≤ 1 ∥ Z I ϕ ( τ , ⋅ ) ∥ L ∞ + C ∫ 0 t ∑ | I | ≤ 2 ( 1 + τ ) − 1 ∥ Z I ϕ ( τ , ⋅ ) ∥ L ∞ d τ \|(1+t+r)\partial\phi(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\int_{0}^{t}(1+\tau)\|\widetilde{\square}_{g}\phi(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}}\,d\tau\\
+C\sup_{0\leq\tau\leq t}\!\sum_{|I|\leq 1}\!\|Z^{I}\!\phi(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}}+C\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{|I|\leq 2}(1+\tau)^{-1}\|Z^{I}\phi(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}}\,d\tau 
 
 
 The estimate ( 2.13 ) will be applied to the
components of the tensor h h . The term Z I ​ h Z^{I}h on the right hand
side of the estimate will be controlled with the help of the weak
decay estimates, and thus the decay rate of h h will be
determined in terms of decay of □ ~ g ​ h \widetilde{\square}_{g}h . The
 L ∞ − L ∞ L^{\infty}-L^{\infty} estimate ( 2.13 ) does not
rely on the fundamental solution as does the more standard
 L 1 − L ∞ L^{1}-L^{\infty} type estimate. This estimate was used [ L1 ] in
the constant coefficient case and here we establish it in the
variable coefficient case only under the assumption of the weak
decay of all of the components of the metric g g and the stronger
decay of the components of g g controlled by the wave coordinate
condition. This analysis is by itself very interesting but we will
not go into it here and just refer the reader to the following
sections. 
 
 
 We now analyze the inhomogeneous term in the equation for h μ ​ ν h_{\mu\nu} .
The tensor
 h μ ​ ν = g μ ​ ν − m μ ​ ν h_{\mu\nu}=g_{\mu\nu}-m_{\mu\nu} verifies the reduced Einstein
equations of the form: 
 

 
 
 
 □ ~ g ​ h μ ​ ν = F μ ​ ν ​ ( h ) ​ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) , \displaystyle\widetilde{\square}_{g}h_{\mu\nu}=F_{\mu\nu}(h)(\partial h,\partial h), 
 
 
 (2.14) 
 
 
 F μ ​ ν ​ ( h ) ​ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) = P ⁡ ( ∂ μ h , ∂ ν h ) + Q μ ​ ν ​ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) + G μ ​ ν ​ ( h ) ​ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) , \displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}(h)(\partial h,\partial h)=P(\partial_{\mu}h,\partial_{\nu}h)+Q_{\mu\nu}(\partial h,\partial h)+G_{\mu\nu}(h)(\partial h,\partial h), 
 
 
 (2.15) 
 
 
 P ⁡ ( ∂ μ h , ∂ ν h ) = 1 4 ​ ∂ μ tr ​ h ​ ∂ ν tr ​ h − 1 2 ​ ∂ μ h α ​ β ​ ∂ ν h α ​ β , \displaystyle P(\partial_{\mu}h,\partial_{\nu}h)=\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\mu}\text{tr}h\,\partial_{\nu}\text{tr}h-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}h^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\nu}h_{\alpha\beta}, 
 
 
 Here Q μ ​ ν Q_{\mu\nu} are linear combinations of the standard null-forms and
 G μ ​ ν ​ ( h ) ​ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) G_{\mu\nu}(h)(\partial h,\partial h) is a quadratic form in ∂ h \partial h with
coefficients as smooth functions of h h vanishing at
 h = 0 h=0 . The weak decay estimates imply that the last two
terms decay fast 
 

 
 (2.16) 
 
 | Q μ ​ ν ​ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) | + | G μ ​ ν ​ ( h ) ​ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) | ≲ | ∂ ¯ ​ h | ​ | ∂ h | + | h | | ∂ h | 2 ≲ ε 2 ​ ( 1 + r + t ) − 2 − 2 ​ γ ​ ( 1 + | t − r | ) − 2 ​ γ |Q_{\mu\nu}(\partial h,\partial h)|+|G_{\mu\nu}(h)(\partial h,\partial h)|\lesssim|\overline{\partial}h|\,|\partial h|+|h||\partial h|^{2}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}(1+r+t)^{-2-2\gamma}(1+|t-r|)^{-2\gamma} 
 
 
 The problematic term is P ⁡ ( ∂ μ h , ∂ ν h ) P(\partial_{\mu}h,\partial_{\nu}h) since a priori the weak decay estimates only give the decay rate of
 ε 2 ​ ( 1 + r + t ) − 1 − 2 ​ γ ​ ( 1 + | t − r | ) − 1 − 2 ​ γ \varepsilon^{2}(1+r+t)^{-1-2\gamma}(1+|t-r|)^{-1-2\gamma} , which is not
sufficient in the wave zone t ≈ r t\approx r .
The crucial improvement comes as a result of a decomposition
of the tensor P ⁡ ( ∂ μ h , ∂ ν h ) P(\partial_{\mu}h,\partial_{\nu}h) with respect to a null
frame { L , L ¯ , S 1 , S 2 } \{L,\underline{L},S_{1},S_{2}\} . Let T ∈ 𝒯 = { L , S 1 , S 2 } T\in{\cal T}=\{L,S_{1},S_{2}\} be
any of the vectors generating the tangent space to the forward
Minkowski light cones and U ∈ 𝒰 = { L , L ¯ , S 1 , S 2 } U\in{\cal U}=\{L,\underline{L},S_{1},S_{2}\} 
denote any of the null frame vectors.
Define, for an arbitrary symmetric two tensor k k ,
 | k | 𝒯𝒰 = ∑ T ∈ 𝒯 , U ∈ 𝒰 = | T μ U μ k μ ​ ν | |k|_{\cal TU}=\sum_{T\in{\cal T},U\in{\cal U}}=|T^{\mu}U^{\mu}k_{\mu\nu}| .
It then follows that 
 

 
 (2.17) 
 
 | P ⁡ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) | 𝒯𝒰 ≲ | ∂ ¯ ​ h | ​ | ∂ h | ≲ ε 2 ​ ( 1 + r + t ) − 2 − 2 ​ γ ​ ( 1 + | t − r | ) − 2 ​ γ |P(\partial h,\partial h)|_{\cal TU}\lesssim|\overline{\partial}h|\,|\partial h|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}(1+r+t)^{-2-2\gamma}(1+|t-r|)^{-2\gamma} 
 
 
 On the other hand, the absolute value of the tensor P ⁡ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) P(\partial h,\partial h) obeys the
estimate 
 

 
 (2.18) 
 
 | P ⁡ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) | ≲ | ∂ h | 𝒯𝒰 2 + | ​ ∂ h | L ​ L ​ | ∂ h | |P(\partial h,\partial h)|\lesssim|\partial h|_{\cal TU}^{2}+|\partial h|_{LL}|\partial h| 
 
 
 We now decompose the system of equations for h h with respect to the
null-frame 
 

 
 (2.19) 
 
 | □ ~ g ​ h | 𝒯𝒰 \displaystyle|\widetilde{\square}_{g}h|_{\cal TU} 
 ≲ ε 2 ​ ( 1 + r + t ) − 2 − 2 ​ γ ​ ( 1 + | t − r | ) − 2 ​ γ , \displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}(1+r+t)^{-2-2\gamma}(1+|t-r|)^{-2\gamma}, 
 
 
 (2.20) 
 
 | □ ~ g ​ h | 𝒰𝒰 \displaystyle|\widetilde{\square}_{g}h|_{\cal UU} 
 ≲ | ∂ h | 𝒯𝒰 2 + ε 2 ​ ( 1 + r + t ) − 2 − 2 ​ γ ​ ( 1 + | t − r | ) − 2 ​ γ , \displaystyle\lesssim|\partial h|_{\cal TU}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}(1+r+t)^{-2-2\gamma}(1+|t-r|)^{-2\gamma}, 
 
 
 where in the last inequality we also used the improved decay
estimate for ∂ h L ​ L \partial h_{LL} obtained from the wave coordinate
condition. The result is a system of equations where the
components □ ~ g ​ h T ​ U \widetilde{\square}_{g}h_{TU} have very good decay properties, while
 □ ~ g ​ h U ​ U \widetilde{\square}_{g}h_{UU} for the remaining non tangential component
depends, to the highest order, only on the components h T ​ U h_{TU} satisfying a better equation.
An additional subtlety in the above analysis is the fact that
contraction with the null frame does not commute with □ ~ g \widetilde{\square}_{g} 
(or even with □ \square ). However, the decay estimate
( 2.13 ) for the wave equation only uses the
principal radial part of □ \square :  ∂ t 2 − r − 2 ∂ r 2 − 2 r − 1 ∂ r \partial_{t}^{2}-r^{-2}\partial_{r}^{2}-2\,r^{-1}\partial_{r} , which respects the null frame. This
analysis results in the improved decay estimates 
 

 
 (2.21) 
 
 | ∂ h | T ​ U ≤ C ​ ε ​ ( 1 + t ) − 1 , | ∂ h | ≤ C ​ ε ​ ( 1 + t ) − 1 ​ ln ⁡ ( 2 + t ) |\partial h|_{TU}\leq C\varepsilon(1+t)^{-1},\qquad|\partial h|\leq C\varepsilon(1+t)^{-1}\ln(2+t) 
 
 
 
 
 The energy estimates. We rely on the following energy
estimate for the wave equation, which holds under the assumption
that the above decay estimates hold for the background metric
 g g : for any γ > 0 \gamma>0 
 

 
 (2.22) 
 
 ∫ Σ T | ∂ ϕ | 2 + ∫ 0 T ∫ Σ τ γ ​ | ∂ ¯ ​ ϕ | 2 ( 1 + | t − r | ) 1 + 2 ​ γ ≤ 8 ​ ∫ Σ 0 | ∂ ϕ | 2 + C ​ ε ​ ∫ 0 T ∫ Σ t | ∂ ϕ | 2 1 + t + 16 ​ ∫ 0 T ∫ Σ t | □ ~ g ​ ϕ | ​ | ∂ t ϕ | \int_{\Sigma_{T}}|\partial\phi|^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\frac{\gamma\,\,|\bar{\partial}\phi|^{2}}{(1+|t-r|)^{1+2\gamma}}\leq 8\int_{\Sigma_{0}}|\partial\phi|^{2}+C\varepsilon\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma_{t}}\frac{|\partial\phi|^{2}}{1+t}+16\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma_{t}}|\widetilde{\square}_{g}\phi||\partial_{t}\phi| 
 
 
 This implies that the energy of a solution of the homogeneous
wave equation □ ~ g ​ ϕ = 0 \widetilde{\square}_{g}\phi=0 grows but
at the rate of at most ( 1 + t ) C ​ ε (1+t)^{C\varepsilon} .
The presence of an additional space-time integral containing tangential derivatives
on the right and side of ( 2.22 ) is crucial for our analysis.
This type of estimate in the constant coefficient case basically follows
by averaging of the energy estimates on light cones used e.g. in [ S1 ] .
We also note that the energy estimates with space-time quantities involving
special derivatives of a solution were also considered and used in the work of Alinhac, see
e.g. [ A2 ] , [ A3 ] ). In our work we use the space-time integral
with derivatives spanning the tangent space to outgoing light cones and weights
dependent on the distance to the Minkowski light cone r = t + 1 r=t+1 .
We emphasize that the
energy estimate ( 2.22 ) is proved only under the assumption of the
weak decay of all components of the background metric g g together with
the strong decay of the components controlled from the wave coordinate condition. 
 
 
 It is worth noting that a combination of the energy estimates of the type
( 2.22 ) and Klainerman-Sobolev inequalities would also yield a very simple
proof of the small data global existence result for semilinear equations
 □ ​ ϕ = Q ⁡ ( ∂ ϕ , ∂ ϕ ) \square\phi=Q(\partial\phi,\partial\phi) obeying the standard null condition.
This fact appears to be previously unknown. 
 
 
 The energy estimate ( 2.22 ) will be applied simultaneously
to all components of the tensor h h .
As in the equations ( 2.19 ), ( 2.20 ) the inhomogeneous term obeys the following estimate: 
 

 
 
 | □ ~ g h | ≲ ε ( 1 + r + t ) − 3 / 2 − γ ( 1 + | t − r | ) 1 / 2 − γ | ∂ h | + ε ( 1 + t ) − 1 | ∂ h | , |\widetilde{\square}_{g}h|\lesssim\varepsilon(1+r+t)^{-3/2-\gamma}(1+|t-r|)^{1/2-\gamma}|\partial h|+\varepsilon(1+t)^{-1}|\partial h|, 
 
 
 where in the last inequality we used the improved decay estimate for
the | ∂ h | 𝒯𝒰 |\partial h|_{\cal TU} components.
The energy estimate ( 11.3 ) will then imply that 
 

 
 
 E 0 ​ ( t ) ≤ 16 ​ ε 2 ​ ( 1 + t ) C ​ ε . E_{0}(t)\leq 16\varepsilon^{2}(1+t)^{C\varepsilon}. 
 
 
 
 
 Higher order energy estimates. 
In addition to the energy
estimates for the components of the tensor h h we need estimates
for the higher vector field derivatives of h h : Z I ​ h Z^{I}h with
Minkowski vector fields Z = { ∂ α , Ω α ​ β , S } Z=\{\partial_{\alpha},\Omega_{\alpha\beta},S\} . To
obtain these estimates we apply Z I Z^{I} to the equation □ ~ g ​ h μ ​ ν = F μ ​ ν \widetilde{\square}_{g}h_{\mu\nu}=F_{\mu\nu} for h h . Applying vector fields to the
nonlinear terms F μ ​ ν F_{\mu\nu} yields similar nonlinear terms for
higher derivatives and these are can be dealt with using the
estimates already described above. We must note however that this
is where the additional space-time integral involving the
tangential derivatives on the left hand side of the energy
estimate ( 11.3 ) becomes crucial. Consider for
example the term ∂ h ⋅ ∂ ¯ ​ Z I ​ h \partial h\cdot\bar{\partial}Z^{I}h generated by one of the
null forms in F μ ​ ν F_{\mu\nu} . We estimate its contribution, with the
help of the weak decay estimates, to the energy estimate as
follows: 
 

 
 
 | ∂ h ​ ‖ ∂ ¯ ​ Z I ​ h ‖ ​ ∂ t Z I ​ h | ≤ C ​ ε ​ | ∂ t Z I ​ h | ( 1 + t ) 1 / 2 + γ ​ | ∂ ¯ ​ Z I ​ h | ( 1 + | t − r | ) 1 / 2 + γ ≤ C ​ ε ​ | ∂ t Z I ​ h | 2 ( 1 + t ) 1 + 2 ​ γ + C ​ ε ​ | ∂ ¯ ​ Z I ​ h | 2 ( 1 + | t − r | ) 1 + 2 ​ γ |\partial h|\,|\bar{\partial}Z^{I}h||\partial_{t}Z^{I}h|\leq\frac{C\varepsilon|\partial_{t}Z^{I}h|}{(1+t)^{1/2+\gamma}}\frac{|\bar{\partial}Z^{I}h|}{(1+|t-r|)^{1/2+\gamma}}\leq\frac{C\varepsilon|\partial_{t}Z^{I}h|^{2}}{(1+t)^{1+2\gamma}}+\frac{C\varepsilon|\bar{\partial}Z^{I}h|^{2}}{(1+|t-r|)^{1+2\gamma}} 
 
 
 The integral of the first term is easily controlled by the
energy on time slices times an integrable factor in time.
The space time integral of the second term is in fact part of the energy
( 2.22 ), and if we choose ε \varepsilon sufficiently small this
term can be absorbed by the space time integral on the left.
The idea with the space-time integral is that one can use the extra
decay in | t − r | |t-r| when one does not have full decay in t t . 
 
 
 The more serious problem in higher order energy estimates lies
however in the commutators between Z I Z^{I} and the principal part
 □ ~ g = g α ​ β ∂ α ∂ β \widetilde{\square}_{g}=g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta} . 
 
 
 The commutators. Writing

commutator estimate 13 13

 This commutator estimate applies to
the vector fields Z = { ∂ α , Ω α ​ β } Z=\{\partial_{\alpha},\Omega_{\alpha\beta}\} . For the scaling
vector field Z = S = x α ∂ α Z=S=x^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha} the commutator expression should
have the form □ ~ g ​ S − ( S + 2 ) ​ □ ~ g \widetilde{\square}_{g}S-(S+2)\widetilde{\square}_{g} . 
 

 
 (2.23) 
 
 | [ Z , □ ~ g ] ​ ϕ | ≤ C ⁡ ( | H | + | Z ​ H | 1 + t + r + | Z ​ H | L ​ L + | H | L ​ 𝒯 1 + | t − r | ) ​ ∑ | I | ≤ 1 | ∂ Z I ​ ϕ | ≤ C ​ ε 1 + t + r ​ ∑ | I | ≤ 1 | ∂ Z I ​ ϕ | \big|[Z,\widetilde{\square}_{g}]\phi\big|\leq C\Big(\frac{|H|+|ZH|}{1+t+r}+\frac{|ZH|_{LL}+|H|_{L\cal T}}{1+|\,t-r|}\Big)\sum_{|I|\leq 1}|\partial Z^{I}\phi|\leq\frac{C\varepsilon}{1+t+r}\sum_{|I|\leq 1}|\partial Z^{I}\phi| 
 
 
 by the weak decay assumptions ( 2.10 ) and the improved decay
from the wave coordinate condition
( 2.12 ). We should note that for a generic quasilinear wave

uncontrollable error terms. In the special case of the equation
 □ ​ ϕ = ϕ ​ Δ ​ ϕ \square\phi=\phi\Delta\phi 
this problem can be overcome by modifying the vector fields Z Z , [ A3 ] .
In our case it is the wave coordinate gauge that provides additional cancellations. 
 
 
 This commutator estimate applied to ϕ = h α ​ β \phi=h_{\alpha\beta} 
together with the analysis in the previous section now gives estimates for the
energy E 1 E_{1} as well as for the stronger decay estimates for the
second derivatives of h h , ( 2.26 ) with | J | = 1 |J|=1 .
This commutator
will also show up as a top order term [ Z , □ ~ g ] ⋅ Z I − 1 ​ h α ​ β [Z,\widetilde{\square}_{g}]\cdot Z^{I-1}h_{\alpha\beta} 
in the energy estimate for Z I ​ h Z^{I}h 
and the resulting term can be dealt with in the same way. 
 
 
 The other top order term generated by the commutators [ Z I , □ ~ g ] ​ ϕ [Z^{I},\widetilde{\square}_{g}]\phi 
is of the form ( Z I H α ​ β ) ∂ α ∂ β (Z^{I}H^{\alpha\beta})\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta} .
We first apply the pointwise estimate 
 

 
 
 | ( Z I ​ H α ​ β ) ​ ∂ α ∂ β ϕ | ≤ C ⁡ ( | Z I ​ H | 1 + t + r + | Z I ​ H | L ​ L 1 + | t − r | ) ​ ∑ | K | ≤ 1 | ∂ Z K ​ ϕ | |(Z^{I}H^{\alpha\beta})\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\phi|\leq C\Big(\frac{|Z^{I}H|}{1+t+r}+\frac{|Z^{I}H|_{LL}}{1+|\,t-r|}\Big)\sum_{|K|\leq 1}|\partial Z^{K}\phi| 
 
 
 To deal with its contribution to the energy estimate
we use the Poincare estimate with a boundary term 
 

 
 (2.24) 
 
 ∫ R 3 | Z I ​ H | L ​ L 2 ​ d ​ x ( 1 + | t − r | ) 2 + 2 ​ σ ≤ C ∫ S ( t + 1 ) | Z I H | L ​ L 2 d S + C ∫ R 3 | ∂ r Z I ​ H | L ​ L 2 ​ d ​ x ( 1 + | t − r | ) 2 ​ σ , σ > − 1 / 2 , σ ≠ 1 / 2 \int\limits_{R^{3}}\frac{|Z^{I}H|_{LL}^{2}\,dx}{(1+|t-r|)^{2+2\sigma}}\leq C\int\limits_{S_{(t+1)}}|Z^{I}H|_{LL}^{2}\,dS+C\int\limits_{R^{3}}\frac{|\partial_{r}Z^{I}H|_{LL}^{2}\,dx}{(1+|t-r|)^{2\sigma}},\qquad\sigma>-1/2,\quad\sigma\neq 1/2 
 
 
 together with the fact that h h is Schwarzschild outside the cone
 r = t + 1 r=t+1 , because of the inward bending of the Schwarzschild light
cones, and hence there | Z I ​ h | ≤ C ​ ε / ( 1 + t ) |Z^{I}h|\leq C\varepsilon/(1+t) . The way
coordinate condition implies that | ∂ Z I ​ H | L ​ L |\partial Z^{I}H|_{LL} can be
estimated by | ∂ ¯ ​ Z I ​ H | |\bar{\partial}Z^{I}H| and lower order terms. The term
involving | ∂ ¯ ​ Z I ​ H | |\bar{\partial}Z^{I}H| is then controlled by the space-time
integral on the left hand side. 
 
 
 One can use a similar but more trivial argument
for decay estimates, i.e. 
 

 
 
 | Z I ​ H | L ​ L ≤ | Z I ​ H L ​ L | r = t + 1 + ( 1 + | t − r | ) ​ | ∂ r Z I ​ H L ​ L | L ∞ |Z^{I}H|_{LL}\leq{|Z^{I}H_{LL}|}_{r=t+1}+(1+|t-r|){|\partial_{r}Z^{I}H_{LL}|}_{L^{\infty}} 
 
 
 
 
 The lower order terms. So far we have only discussed the top
order terms, but there will also be several lower order terms
(relative to | I | = k + 1 |I|=k+1 ) to deal with. These are typically of the
form 
 

 
 (2.25) 
 
 | ∂ Z J ​ h | ​ | ∂ Z K ​ h | or | Z J ​ h | ​ | ∂ 2 Z K − 1 ​ h | ≤ C ​ | Z J ​ h | 1 + | t − r | ​ | ∂ Z K ​ h | |\partial Z^{J}h|\,|\partial Z^{K}h|\qquad\text{or}\qquad|Z^{J}h|\,|\partial^{2}Z^{K-1}h|\leq C\frac{|Z^{J}h|}{1+|t-r|}\,|\partial Z^{K}h| 
 
 
 with | J | , | K | < | I | = k + 1 |J|,|K|<|I|=k+1 The lower order terms are dealt with using induction.
We describe the induction argument for the decay estimates. From
this it will be clear how it also proceeds for the energy
estimates. We will inductively assume that we have the bounds: 
 

 
 (2.26) 
 
 | ∂ Z J ​ h | + | Z J ​ h | ​ ( 1 + | t − r | ) − 1 ≤ C k ​ t − 1 + C k ​ ε , | J | ≤ k |\partial Z^{J}h|+|Z^{J}h|(1+|t-r|)^{-1}\leq C_{k}t^{-1+C_{k}\varepsilon},\qquad|J|\leq k 
 
 
 The terms in
( 2.25 ) can then be estimated by C k 2 ​ ε 2 ​ t − 2 + 2 ​ C k ​ ε C_{k}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}t^{-2+2C_{k}\varepsilon} . Including the top order terms using
( 2.23 ) applied to ϕ = Z I − 1 ​ h \phi=Z^{I-1}h , and
using ( 2.13 ) applied to □ ~ g ​ Z I ​ h \widetilde{\square}_{g}Z^{I}h we
get an inequality of the form 
 

 
 (2.27) 
 
 M ⁡ ( t ) ≤ ∫ 0 t C ​ ε ​ M ​ ( s ) 1 + s + C ​ ε 2 ( 1 + s ) 1 − C ​ ε ​ 𝑑 s M(t)\leq\int_{0}^{t}\frac{C\varepsilon\,M(s)}{1+s}\,+\frac{C\varepsilon^{2}}{(1+s)^{1-C\varepsilon}}\,ds 
 
 
 where M ⁡ ( t ) = ( 1 + t ) ​ ‖ ∂ Z I ​ h ​ ( t , ⋅ ) ‖ L ∞ M(t)=(1+t)\|\partial Z^{I}h(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}} . The Gronwall’s inequality then gives
the bound M ⁡ ( t ) ≤ C ​ ( 1 + t ) 2 ​ C ​ ε M(t)\leq C(1+t)^{2C\varepsilon} . 
 
 
 
 3 The Einstein equations in wave coordinates 

 
 For a Lorentzian metric g μ ​ ν g_{\mu\nu} , where μ , ν = 0 , … , 3 \mu,\nu=0,...,3 
we denote 
 

 
 (3.1) 
 
 Γ μ ​ ν λ = 1 2 ​ g λ ​ δ ​ ( ∂ μ g δ ​ ν + ∂ ν g δ ​ μ − ∂ δ g μ ​ ν ) , \Gamma_{\mu\,\,\,\nu}^{\,\,\,\lambda}=\frac{1}{2}g^{\lambda\delta}\big(\partial_{\mu}g_{\delta\nu}+\partial_{\nu}g_{\delta\mu}-\partial_{\delta}g_{\mu\nu}\big), 
 
 
 the Christoffel symbols of g g and 
 

 
 (3.2) 
 
 R μ ​ ν ​ δ λ = ∂ δ Γ μ ​ ν λ − ∂ ν Γ μ ​ δ λ + Γ ρ ​ δ λ ​ Γ μ ​ ν ρ − Γ ρ ​ ν λ ​ Γ μ ​ δ ρ R_{\mu\,\,\,\nu\delta}^{\,\,\,\lambda}=\partial_{\delta}\Gamma_{\mu\,\,\,\nu}^{\,\,\,\lambda}-\partial_{\nu}\Gamma_{\mu\,\,\,\delta}^{\,\,\,\lambda}+\Gamma_{\rho\,\,\,\delta}^{\,\,\,\lambda}\Gamma_{\mu\,\,\,\nu}^{\,\,\,\rho}-\Gamma_{\rho\,\,\,\nu}^{\,\,\,\lambda}\Gamma_{\mu\,\,\,\delta}^{\,\,\,\rho} 
 
 
 its Riemann curvature tensor with
 R μ ​ ν = R μ ​ ν ​ α α R_{\mu\nu}=R_{\mu\,\,\,\nu\alpha}^{\,\,\,\alpha} ,
the Ricci tensor. 
 
 
 We consider the metric g g satisfying the Einstein vacuum equations 
 

 
 (3.3) 
 
 R μ ​ ν = 0 . R_{\mu\nu}=0. 
 
 
 We impose the wave coordinate condition: 
 

 
 (3.4) 
 
 Γ λ := g α ​ β ​ Γ α ​ β λ = 0 \Gamma^{\lambda}:=g^{\alpha\beta}\,\Gamma_{\alpha\,\,\,\beta}^{\,\,\,\lambda}=0 
 
 
 It follows that assuming ( 3.4 ) we have that
the reduced wave operator □ ~ g = g α ​ β \widetilde{\square}_{g}=g^{\alpha\beta} 
 

 
 (3.5) 
 
 □ ~ g = □ g = 1 | g | ∂ α g α ​ β | g | ∂ β \widetilde{\square}_{g}={\square}_{g}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|g|}}\partial_{\alpha}g^{\alpha\beta}\sqrt{|g|}\partial_{\beta} 
 
 
 The following lemma provides the description of the Einstein vacuum
equations in wave coordinates as a system of quasilinear wave equations
for g μ ​ ν g_{\mu\nu} . 
 
 
 Lemma 3.1 . 
 
 Let metric g g satisfy the Einstein vacuum equations ( 3.3 ) together with
the wave coordinate condition ( 3.4 ).
Then g μ ​ ν g_{\mu\nu} solves the following system of reduced Einstein equations: 
 

 
 (3.6) 
 
 □ ~ g ​ g μ ​ ν = P ~ ​ ( ∂ μ g , ∂ ν g ) + Q ~ μ ​ ν ​ ( ∂ g , ∂ g ) {\widetilde{\square}}_{g}g_{\mu\nu}=\widetilde{P}(\partial_{\mu}g,\partial_{\nu}g)+\widetilde{Q}_{\mu\nu}(\partial g,\partial g) 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 (3.7) 
 
 
 P ~ ​ ( ∂ μ g , ∂ ν g ) = 1 4 ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ ∂ μ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ∂ ν g β ​ β ′ − 1 2 ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ∂ μ g α ​ β ​ ∂ ν g α ′ ​ β ′ \displaystyle\widetilde{P}(\partial_{\mu}g,\partial_{\nu}g)=\frac{1}{4}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\,g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\nu}g_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha\beta}\,\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}} 
 
 
 (3.8) 
 
 
 Q ~ μ ​ ν ​ ( ∂ g , ∂ g ) = ∂ α g β ​ μ ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α ′ g β ′ ​ ν − g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( ∂ α g β ​ μ ​ ∂ β ′ g α ′ ​ ν − ∂ β ′ g β ​ μ ​ ∂ α g α ′ ​ ν ) \displaystyle\widetilde{Q}_{\mu\nu}(\partial g,\partial g)=\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}\,\,g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}g_{\beta^{\prime}\nu}-g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}\,\,\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\nu}-\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\beta\mu}\,\,\partial_{\alpha}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\nu}\big) 
 
 
 
 
 + g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( ∂ μ g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α g β ​ ν − ∂ α g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ μ g β ​ ν ) + g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( ∂ ν g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α g β ​ μ − ∂ α g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ ν g β ​ μ ) \displaystyle\qquad+g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\nu}-\partial_{\alpha}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\nu}\big)+g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}-\partial_{\alpha}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\nu}g_{\beta\mu}\big) 
 
 
 
 
 + 1 2 ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( ∂ β ′ g α ​ α ′ ​ ∂ μ g β ​ ν − ∂ μ g α ​ α ′ ​ ∂ β ′ g β ​ ν ) + 1 2 ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( ∂ β ′ g α ​ α ′ ​ ∂ ν g β ​ μ − ∂ ν g α ​ α ′ ​ ∂ β ′ g β ​ μ ) \displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\nu}-\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\beta\nu}\big)+\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\partial_{\nu}g_{\beta\mu}-\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\beta\mu}\big) 
 
 
 Furthermore, the wave coordinate condition ( 3.4 ) reads 
 

 
 (3.9) 
 
 g α ​ β ​ ∂ α g β ​ μ = 1 2 ​ g α ​ β ​ ∂ μ g α ​ β , or ∂ α g α ​ ν = 1 2 ​ g α ​ β ​ g ν ​ μ ​ ∂ μ g α ​ β g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}=\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha\beta},\qquad\text{or}\qquad\partial_{\alpha}g^{\alpha\nu}=\frac{1}{2}g_{\alpha\beta}g^{\nu\mu}\partial_{\mu}g^{\alpha\beta} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof of ( 3.9 ) is immediate. 
 
 
 We now observe that 
 

 
 
 ∂ α g β ​ μ = Γ α ​ β ​ μ + Γ α ​ μ ​ β , where Γ μ ​ α ​ ν = g α ​ λ ​ Γ μ ​ ν λ . \partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}=\Gamma_{\alpha\beta\mu}+\Gamma_{\alpha\mu\beta},\qquad\quad\text{where}\qquad\Gamma_{\mu\alpha\nu}=g_{\alpha\lambda}\Gamma_{\mu\,\,\,\nu}^{\,\,\,\lambda}. 
 
 
 It follows that g α ​ λ ​ ∂ β Γ μ ​ ν λ = ∂ β Γ μ ​ α ​ ν − ( Γ β ​ α ​ λ + Γ β ​ λ ​ α ) ​ Γ μ ​ ν λ g_{\alpha\lambda}\partial_{\beta}\Gamma_{\mu\,\,\,\nu}^{\,\,\,\lambda}=\partial_{\beta}\Gamma_{\mu\alpha\nu}-(\Gamma_{\beta\alpha\lambda}+\Gamma_{\beta\lambda\alpha})\Gamma_{\mu\,\,\,\nu}^{\,\,\,\lambda} so also using that
 Γ α ​ λ ​ β = Γ β ​ λ ​ α \Gamma_{\alpha\lambda\beta}=\Gamma_{\beta\lambda\alpha} we obtain 
 

 
 (3.10) 
 
 R μ ​ α ​ ν ​ β = g α ​ λ ​ R μ ​ ν ​ β λ = ∂ β Γ μ ​ α ​ ν − ∂ ν Γ μ ​ α ​ β + Γ ν ​ λ ​ α ​ Γ μ ​ β λ − Γ α ​ λ ​ β ​ Γ μ ​ ν λ R_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}=g_{\alpha\lambda}R_{\mu\,\,\,\nu\beta}^{\,\,\,\lambda}=\partial_{\beta}\Gamma_{\mu\alpha\nu}-\partial_{\nu}\Gamma_{\mu\alpha\beta}+\Gamma_{\nu\lambda\alpha}\Gamma_{\mu\,\,\,\beta}^{\,\,\,\lambda}-\Gamma_{\alpha\lambda\beta}\Gamma_{\mu\,\,\,\nu}^{\,\,\,\lambda} 
 
 
 It follows from ( 3.9 ) that 
 

 
 (3.11) 
 
 g α ​ β ( ∂ μ ∂ α g β ​ ν − 1 2 ∂ μ ∂ ν g α ​ β ) = − ∂ μ g α ​ β ( ∂ α g β ​ ν − 1 2 ∂ ν g α ​ β ) = g α ​ α ′ g β ​ β ′ ∂ μ g α ′ ​ β ′ ( ∂ α g β ​ ν − 1 2 ∂ ν g α ​ β ) g^{\alpha\beta}\Big(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha\beta}\Big)=-\partial_{\mu}g^{\alpha\beta}\Big(\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha\beta}\Big)=g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\Big(\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha\beta}\Big) 
 
 
 and hence 
 

 
 (3.12) 
 
 g α ​ β ​ ( ∂ α Γ μ ​ β ​ ν − ∂ ν Γ μ ​ β ​ α ) = g α ​ β 2 ​ ( ∂ α ∂ μ g β ​ ν + ∂ α ∂ ν g β ​ μ − ∂ α ∂ β g μ ​ ν ) − g α ​ β 2 ​ ( ∂ ν ∂ μ g β ​ α + ∂ ν ∂ α g β ​ μ − ∂ ν ∂ β g μ ​ α ) = − g α ​ β 2 ∂ α ∂ β g μ ​ ν + g α ​ β 2 ( ∂ α ∂ μ g β ​ ν + ∂ ν ∂ β g μ ​ α − ∂ ν ∂ μ g β ​ α ) = − 1 2 g α ​ β ∂ α ∂ β g μ ​ ν + 1 2 g α ​ α ′ g β ​ β ′ ( ∂ μ g α ′ ​ β ′ ∂ α g β ​ ν + ∂ ν g α ′ ​ β ′ ∂ α g β ​ μ − ∂ ν g α ′ ​ β ′ ∂ μ g α ​ β ) . g^{\alpha\beta}\big(\partial_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\mu\beta\nu}-\partial_{\nu}\Gamma_{\mu\beta\alpha}\big)=\frac{g^{\alpha\beta}}{2}\big(\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\nu}+\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\nu}g_{\beta\mu}-\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}g_{\mu\nu}\big)-\frac{g^{\alpha\beta}}{2}\big(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\alpha}+\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}-\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\beta}g_{\mu\alpha}\big)\\
=-\frac{g^{\alpha\beta}}{2}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}g_{\mu\nu}+\frac{g^{\alpha\beta}}{2}\big(\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\nu}+\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\beta}g_{\mu\alpha}-\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\alpha}\big)\\
=-\frac{1}{2}\,g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}g_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\big(\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\nu}+\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}-\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha\beta}\big). 
 
 
 Here by ( 3.9 ) we can write 
 

 
 (3.13) 
 
 g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ∂ μ g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α g β ​ ν = g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ μ g β ​ ν + g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( ∂ μ g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α g β ​ ν − ∂ α g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ μ g β ​ ν ) = 1 2 ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ∂ β ′ g α ′ ​ α ​ ∂ μ g β ​ ν + g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( ∂ μ g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α g β ​ ν − ∂ α g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ μ g β ​ ν ) = 1 2 ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ∂ μ g α ′ ​ α ​ ∂ β ′ g β ​ ν + g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( 1 2 ​ ( ∂ β ′ g α ′ ​ α ​ ∂ μ g β ​ ν − ∂ μ g α ′ ​ α ​ ∂ β ′ g β ​ ν ) + ( ∂ μ g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α g β ​ ν − ∂ α g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ μ g β ​ ν ) ) = 1 4 ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ∂ μ g α ′ ​ α ​ ∂ ν g β ​ β ′ + g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( 1 2 ​ ( ∂ β ′ g α ′ ​ α ​ ∂ μ g β ​ ν − ∂ μ g α ′ ​ α ​ ∂ β ′ g β ​ ν ) + ( ∂ μ g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α g β ​ ν − ∂ α g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ μ g β ​ ν ) ) g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\nu}=g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\alpha}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\nu}+g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\big(\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\nu}-\partial_{\alpha}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\nu}\big)\\
=\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\nu}+g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\big(\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\nu}-\partial_{\alpha}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\nu}\big)\\
=\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\beta\nu}+g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\big(\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\nu}-\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\beta\nu}\big)+\big(\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\nu}-\partial_{\alpha}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\nu}\big)\Big)\\
=\frac{1}{4}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,\partial_{\nu}g_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}+g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\big(\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\nu}-\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\beta\nu}\big)+\big(\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\nu}-\partial_{\alpha}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\nu}\big)\Big) 
 
 
 Hence by ( 3.13 )
and ( 3.13 ) with μ \mu and ν \nu interchanged
we get 
 

 
 (3.14) 
 
 1 2 ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( ∂ μ g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α g β ​ ν + ∂ ν g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α g β ​ μ − ∂ ν g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ μ g α ​ β ) = g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( 1 4 ​ ∂ μ g α ′ ​ α ​ ∂ ν g β ​ β ′ − 1 2 ​ ∂ ν g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ μ g α ​ β ) + 1 2 ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( ( ∂ μ g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α g β ​ ν − ∂ α g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ μ g β ​ ν ) + ( ∂ ν g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α g β ​ μ − ∂ α g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ ν g β ​ μ ) ) 1 4 ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( ( ∂ β ′ g α ′ ​ α ​ ∂ μ g β ​ ν − ∂ μ g α ′ ​ α ​ ∂ β ′ g β ​ ν ) + ( ∂ β ′ g α ′ ​ α ​ ∂ ν g β ​ μ − ∂ ν g α ′ ​ α ​ ∂ β ′ g β ​ μ ) ) \frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\Big(\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\nu}+\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}-\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha\beta}\Big)=g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\big(\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,\partial_{\nu}g_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha\beta}\big)\\
+\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\Big(\big(\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\nu}-\partial_{\alpha}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\nu}\big)+\big(\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}-\partial_{\alpha}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\nu}g_{\beta\mu}\big)\Big)\\
\frac{1}{4}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\Big(\big(\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\nu}-\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\beta\nu}\big)+\big(\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,\partial_{\nu}g_{\beta\mu}-\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\beta\mu}\big)\Big) 
 
 
 On the other hand 
 

 
 (3.15) 
 
 Γ ν ​ α ​ β ​ Γ μ α ​ β = 1 4 ​ ( ∂ ν g β ​ α + ∂ β g α ​ ν − ∂ α g β ​ ν ) ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( ∂ μ g β ′ ​ α ′ + ∂ β ′ g α ′ ​ μ − ∂ α ′ g β ′ ​ μ ) = 1 4 ​ ∂ ν g α ​ β ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ∂ μ g α ′ ​ β ′ + 1 2 ​ ∂ α g β ​ μ ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α ′ g β ′ ​ ν − 1 2 ​ ∂ α g β ​ μ ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ∂ β ′ g α ′ ​ ν = g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( 1 4 ​ ∂ ν g α ​ β ​ ∂ μ g α ′ ​ β ′ + 1 2 ​ ∂ α g β ​ μ ​ ∂ α ′ g β ′ ​ ν − 1 2 ​ ∂ β ′ g β ​ μ ​ ∂ α g α ′ ​ ν ) − 1 2 ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( ∂ α g β ​ μ ​ ∂ β ′ g α ′ ​ ν − ∂ β ′ g β ​ μ ​ ∂ α g α ′ ​ ν ) = g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( 1 4 ​ ∂ ν g α ​ β ​ ∂ μ g α ′ ​ β ′ − 1 8 ​ ∂ μ g β ​ β ′ ​ ∂ ν g α ​ α ′ + 1 2 ​ ∂ α g β ​ μ ​ ∂ α ′ g β ′ ​ ν ) − 1 2 ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( ∂ α g β ​ μ ​ ∂ β ′ g α ′ ​ ν − ∂ β ′ g β ​ μ ​ ∂ α g α ′ ​ ν ) \Gamma_{\nu\alpha\beta}\Gamma_{\mu}^{\,\,\,\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{4}\big(\partial_{\nu}g_{\beta\alpha}+\partial_{\beta}g_{\alpha\nu}-\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\nu}\big)g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}+\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\mu}-\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}g_{\beta^{\prime}\mu}\big)\\
=\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha\beta}\,\,g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}\,\,g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}g_{\beta^{\prime}\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}\,\,g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\nu}\\
=g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\Big(\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha\beta}\,\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}\,\,\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}g_{\beta^{\prime}\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\beta\mu}\,\,\partial_{\alpha}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\nu}\Big)\\
-\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\Big(\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}\,\,\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\nu}-\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\beta\mu}\,\,\partial_{\alpha}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\nu}\Big)\\
=g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\Big(\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha\beta}\,\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{8}\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\,\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}\,\,\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}g_{\beta^{\prime}\nu}\Big)\\
-\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\Big(\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}\,\,\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\nu}-\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\beta\mu}\,\,\partial_{\alpha}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\nu}\Big) 
 
 
 where the last inequality follows from ( 3.9 ). 
 
 
 Taking the trace of ( 3.10 ) and using ( 3.12 ),
( 3.4 ) we obtain 
 

 
 (3.16) 
 
 R μ ​ ν = − 1 2 g α ​ β ∂ α ∂ β g μ ​ ν + Γ ν ​ α ​ β Γ μ α ​ β + 1 2 g α ​ α ′ g β ​ β ′ ( ∂ μ g α ′ ​ β ′ ∂ α g β ​ ν + ∂ ν g α ′ ​ β ′ ∂ α g β ​ μ − ∂ ν g α ′ ​ β ′ ∂ μ g α ​ β ) , R_{\mu\nu}=-\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}g_{\mu\nu}+\Gamma_{\nu\alpha\beta}\Gamma_{\mu}^{\,\,\,\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\Big(\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\nu}+\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}-\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha\beta}\Big), 
 
 
 Using ( 3.15 ) and ( 3.14 )
we get 
 

 
 (3.17) 
 
 R μ ​ ν = − 1 2 g α ​ β ∂ α ∂ β g μ ​ ν + g α ​ α ′ g β ​ β ′ ( − 1 4 ∂ ν g α ​ β ∂ μ g α ′ ​ β ′ + 1 8 ∂ μ g β ​ β ′ ∂ ν g α ​ α ′ ) + 1 2 g α ​ α ′ g β ​ β ′ ∂ α g β ​ μ ∂ α ′ g β ′ ​ ν − 1 2 g α ​ α ′ g β ​ β ′ ( ∂ α g β ​ μ ∂ β ′ g α ′ ​ ν − ∂ β ′ g β ​ μ ∂ α g α ′ ​ ν ) + 1 2 ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( ( ∂ μ g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α g β ​ ν − ∂ α g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ μ g β ​ ν ) + ( ∂ ν g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α g β ​ μ − ∂ α g α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ ν g β ​ μ ) ) 1 4 ​ g α ​ α ′ ​ g β ​ β ′ ​ ( ( ∂ β ′ g α ′ ​ α ​ ∂ μ g β ​ ν − ∂ μ g α ′ ​ α ​ ∂ β ′ g β ​ ν ) + ( ∂ β ′ g α ′ ​ α ​ ∂ ν g β ​ μ − ∂ ν g α ′ ​ α ​ ∂ β ′ g β ​ μ ) ) R_{\mu\nu}=-\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}g_{\mu\nu}+g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\Big(-\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha\beta}\,\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{8}\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\,\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\Big)\\
+\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}\,\,\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}g_{\beta^{\prime}\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\Big(\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}\,\,\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\nu}-\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\beta\mu}\,\,\partial_{\alpha}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\nu}\Big)\\
+\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,\Big(\big(\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\nu}-\partial_{\alpha}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\nu}\big)+\big(\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}-\partial_{\alpha}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\nu}g_{\beta\mu}\big)\Big)\\
\frac{1}{4}g^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\Big(\big(\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,\partial_{\mu}g_{\beta\nu}-\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\beta\nu}\big)+\big(\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,\partial_{\nu}g_{\beta\mu}-\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\beta\mu}\big)\Big) 
 
 
 The result now follows.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Let m m denote the standard Minkowski metric 
 

 
 
 m 00 = − 1 , m i ​ i = 1 , if i = 1 , … , 3 , and m μ ​ ν = 0 , if μ ≠ ν , m_{00}=-1,\qquad m_{ii}=1,\quad\text{if}\quad i=1,...,3,\quad\text{and}\quad m_{\mu\nu}=0,\quad\text{if}\quad\mu\neq\nu, 
 
 
 Define a 2-tensor h h from the decomposition 
 

 
 
 g μ ​ ν = m μ ​ ν + h μ ​ ν . g_{\mu\nu}=m_{\mu\nu}+h_{\mu\nu}. 
 
 
 Let m μ ​ ν m^{\mu\nu} be the inverse of m μ ​ ν m_{\mu\nu} .
Then for small h h 
 

 
 
 H μ ​ ν = g μ ​ ν − m μ ​ ν = − h μ ​ ν + O μ ​ ν ​ ( h 2 ) , where h μ ​ ν = m μ ​ μ ′ ​ m ν ​ ν ′ ​ h μ ′ ​ ν ′ H^{\mu\nu}=g^{\mu\nu}-m^{\mu\nu}=-h^{\mu\nu}+O^{\mu\nu}(h^{2}),\qquad\text{where}\qquad h^{\mu\nu}=m^{\mu\mu^{\prime}}m^{\nu\nu^{\prime}}h_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}} 
 
 
 and O μ ​ ν ​ ( h 2 ) O^{\mu\nu}(h^{2}) vanishes to second order at h = 0 h=0 . 
 
 
 As a consequence of Lemma 3.1 we get: 
 
 
 Lemma 3.2 . 
 
 If Einstein’s equation’s ( 3.3 ) and the wave coordinate
condition ( 3.4 ) hold then 
 

 
 (3.18) 
 
 □ ~ g ​ h μ ​ ν = F μ ​ ν ​ ( h ) ​ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) {\widetilde{\square}}_{g}h_{\mu\nu}=F_{\mu\nu}(h)(\partial h,\partial h)

 F μ ​ ν ​ ( h ) ​ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) F_{\mu\nu}(h)(\partial h,\partial h) is a quadratic form in ∂ h \partial h with

 (3.19)
