 where 
 

 
 (3.20) 
 
 P ⁡ ( ∂ μ h , ∂ ν h ) = 1 4 ​ m α ​ α ′ ​ ∂ μ h α ​ α ′ ​ m β ​ β ′ ​ ∂ ν h β ​ β ′ − 1 2 ​ m α ​ α ′ ​ m β ​ β ′ ​ ∂ μ h α ​ β ​ ∂ ν h α ′ ​ β ′ P(\partial_{\mu}h,\partial_{\nu}h)=\frac{1}{4}m^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\partial_{\mu}h_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\,m^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\nu}h_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}m^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}m^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\mu}h_{\alpha\beta}\,\partial_{\nu}h_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}} 
 
 
 and 
 

 
 (3.21) 
 
 Q μ ​ ν ​ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) = ∂ α h β ​ μ ​ m α ​ α ′ ​ m β ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α ′ h β ′ ​ ν − m α ​ α ′ ​ m β ​ β ′ ​ ( ∂ α h β ​ μ ​ ∂ β ′ h α ′ ​ ν − ∂ β ′ h β ​ μ ​ ∂ α h α ′ ​ ν ) + m α ​ α ′ ​ m β ​ β ′ ​ ( ∂ μ h α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α h β ​ ν − ∂ α h α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ μ h β ​ ν ) + m α ​ α ′ ​ m β ​ β ′ ​ ( ∂ ν h α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ α g ​ h β ​ μ − ∂ α h α ′ ​ β ′ ​ ∂ ν h β ​ μ ) + 1 2 ​ m α ​ α ′ ​ m β ​ β ′ ​ ( ∂ β ′ h α ​ α ′ ​ ∂ μ h β ​ ν − ∂ μ h α ​ α ′ ​ ∂ β ′ h β ​ ν ) + 1 2 ​ m α ​ α ′ ​ m β ​ β ′ ​ ( ∂ β ′ h α ​ α ′ ​ ∂ ν h β ​ μ − ∂ ν h α ​ α ′ ​ ∂ β ′ h β ​ μ ) Q_{\mu\nu}(\partial h,\partial h)=\partial_{\alpha}h_{\beta\mu}\,\,m^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}m^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}h_{\beta^{\prime}\nu}-m^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}m^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial_{\alpha}h_{\beta\mu}\,\,\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}h_{\alpha^{\prime}\nu}-\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}h_{\beta\mu}\,\,\partial_{\alpha}h_{\alpha^{\prime}\nu}\big)\\
+m^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}m^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial_{\mu}h_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\alpha}h_{\beta\nu}-\partial_{\alpha}h_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\mu}h_{\beta\nu}\big)+m^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}m^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial_{\nu}h_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\alpha}gh_{\beta\mu}-\partial_{\alpha}h_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\nu}h_{\beta\mu}\big)\\
+\frac{1}{2}m^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}m^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}h_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\mu}h_{\beta\nu}-\partial_{\mu}h_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}h_{\beta\nu}\big)+\frac{1}{2}m^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}m^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}h_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\nu}h_{\beta\mu}-\partial_{\nu}h_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}h_{\beta\mu}\big) 
 
 
 is a null form and
 G μ ​ ν ​ ( h ) ​ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) G_{\mu\nu}(h)(\partial h,\partial h) is a quadratic form in ∂ h \partial h with
coefficients smoothly dependent on h h and vanishing when h h 
vanishes: G μ ​ ν ​ ( 0 ) ​ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) = 0 G_{\mu\nu}(0)(\partial h,\partial h)=0 . 
 
 
 Furthermore 
 

 
 (3.22) 
 
 m α ​ β ​ ∂ α h β ​ μ = 1 2 ​ m α ​ β ​ ∂ μ h α ​ β + G μ ​ ( h ) ​ ( ∂ h ) , or ∂ α H α ​ ν = 1 2 ​ g α ​ β ​ ( m ν ​ μ + H ν ​ μ ) ​ ∂ μ H α ​ β m^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\beta\mu}=\frac{1}{2}m^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\mu}h_{\alpha\beta}+G_{\mu}(h)(\partial h),\qquad\text{or}\qquad\partial_{\alpha}H^{\alpha\nu}=\frac{1}{2}g_{\alpha\beta}\big(m^{\nu\mu}+H^{\nu\mu}\big)\partial_{\mu}H^{\alpha\beta} 
 
 
 where G μ ​ ( h ) ​ ( ∂ h ) G_{\mu}(h)(\partial h) is a linear function of ∂ h \partial h with coefficients
that are smooth functions of h h and that vanishes when h h vanishes:
 G μ ​ ( 0 ) ​ ( ∂ h ) = 0 G_{\mu}(0)(\partial h)=0 . 
 
 
 
 Observe that the terms in ( 3.20 ) do not satisfy the
classical null condition. However the trace m μ ​ ν ​ h μ ​ ν m^{\mu\nu}h_{\mu\nu} satisfies a nonlinear wave equation with semilinear
terms obeying the the null condition: 
 

 
 
 g α ​ β ​ ∂ α ∂ β m μ ​ ν ​ h μ ​ ν = Q ⁡ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) + G ⁡ ( h ) ​ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) . g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}m^{\mu\nu}h_{\mu\nu}=Q(\partial h,\partial h)+G(h)(\partial h,\partial h). 
 
 
 
 
 
 4 The initial data 

 
 In this section we discuss
the initial data for which the results of our paper apply.
We shall consider the asymptotically flat data, satisfying a
global smallness condition, with the property that it coincides with
the Schwarzschild data outside
the ball of radius one. 
 
 
 We start by showing the existence of such data. Let
 ( g 0 , k 0 ) (g_{0},k_{0}) be asymptotically flat initial data for the
Einstein equations consisting of the Riemannian metric g 0 g_{0} and
a second fundamental form k 0 k_{0} . The initial data for the vacuum
Einstein satisfy the constraint equations 
 

 
 (4.1) 
 
 
 R 0 − ( tr ​ k 0 ) 2 + | k 0 | 2 = 0 , \displaystyle R_{0}-(\text{tr}k_{0})^{2}+|k_{0}|^{2}=0, 
 
 
 (4.2) 
 
 
 ∇ j k 0 i ​ j − ∇ i tr ​ k 0 = 0 \displaystyle\nabla^{j}{k_{0}}_{ij}-\nabla_{i}\text{tr}k_{0}=0 
 
 
 We restrict our attention to the time-symmetric case R 0 = k 0 = 0 R_{0}=k_{0}=0 .
Then, if ( g 0 , k 0 ) (g_{0},k_{0}) is sufficiently close to the Minkowski data and g 0 g_{0} satisfies
the parity condition g 0 ​ ( x ) = g 0 ​ ( − x ) g_{0}(x)=g_{0}(-x) ,
by the results of Corvino [ Co ] and Chrusciel-Delay [ C-D ] one can
construct a new set of initial data ( g , k ) (g,k) with the properties that 
 
 • 
 
 The initial data ( g , k ) (g,k) coincides with ( g 0 , k 0 ) (g_{0},k_{0}) on
the ball of radius 1/2. 
 
 • 
 
 ( g , k ) (g,k) is exactly the Schwarzschild
data ( g S x , 0 ) (g_{S}^{x},0) of mass M M outside B 1 B_{1} , the ball of radius
one. 
 
 
 At this point we specify the smallness conditions: 
 

 
 (4.3) 
 
 M ≤ ϵ , ∑ 0 ≤ | I | ≤ N ( ‖ ∂ x I ( g − δ ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( B 1 ) + ∑ 0 ≤ | J | ≤ N − 1 ‖ ∂ x J k ‖ L 2 ​ ( B 1 ) ) ≤ ϵ M\leq\epsilon,\qquad\sum_{0\leq|I|\leq N}\Big(\|\partial_{x}^{I}(g-\delta)\|_{L^{2}(B_{1})}+\sum_{0\leq|J|\leq N-1}\|\partial^{J}_{x}k\|_{L^{2}(B_{1})}\Big)\leq\epsilon 
 
 
 for some sufficiently large integer N N . Here ∂ x I \partial_{x}^{I} denotes the derivative
 ∂ x 1 I 1 … ∂ x n I n \partial_{x_{1}}^{I_{1}}...\partial_{x_{n}}^{I_{n}} , where
 ( I 1 , . . , I n ) (I_{1},..,I_{n}) is an arbitrary multi-index with the
property that I 1 + . . + I n = | I | I_{1}+..+I_{n}=|I| . 
 
 
 We have two expressions for the Schwarzschild metric
in isotropic and wave coordinates: 
 

 
 (4.4) 
 
 
 g S = − ( 1 − M / r ) 2 ( 1 + M / r ) 2 ​ d ​ t 2 + ( 1 + M r ) 4 ​ d ​ x 2 , \displaystyle g_{S}=-\frac{(1-M/{r})^{2}}{(1+M/{r})^{2}}dt^{2}+(1+\frac{M}{r})^{4}dx^{2}, 
 
 
 (4.5) 
 
 
 g s = − r − 2 ​ M r + 2 ​ M ​ d ​ t 2 + r + 2 ​ M r − 2 ​ M ​ d ​ r 2 + ( r + 2 ​ M ) 2 ​ ( d ​ θ 2 + sin 2 ⁡ θ ​ d ​ ϕ 2 ) \displaystyle g_{s}=-\frac{r-2M}{r+2M}dt^{2}+\frac{r+2M}{r-2M}dr^{2}+(r+2M)^{2}(d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta d\phi^{2}) 
 
 
 The expressions g S x g_{S}^{x} and g s x g_{s}^{x} will denote the spatial parts
the Schwarzschild metric in respective coordinates.
Observe that 
 

 
 (4.6) 
 
 g s = m + 4 ​ M r ​ ( d ​ t 2 + d ​ x 2 ) + O ⁡ ( r − 2 ) , g_{s}=m+\frac{4M}{r}(dt^{2}+dx^{2})+O(r^{-2}), 
 
 
 We now find the coordinate change transforming the metric g S g_{S} 
into g s g_{s} . Set 
 

 
 (4.7) 
 
 t = τ , r = ρ + M 2 ρ t=\tau,\qquad r=\rho+\frac{M^{2}}{\rho} 
 
 
 In the coordinates τ , ρ \tau,\rho the metric g s g_{s} takes the
form g S g_{S} . This change of coordinates is one-to-one for the
values ρ > M \rho>M . Since the mass M << 1 M<<1 we can define
the change of coordinates r = Φ ⁡ ( ρ ) r=\Phi(\rho) , where Φ \Phi coincides
with the map ( 4.7 ) for ρ > 1 \rho>1 and the identity
transformation for ρ ≤ 1 / 2 \rho\leq 1/2 .
Thus we have constructed the initial data ( g , k ) (g,k) such that 
 
 • 
 
 The initial data ( g , k ) (g,k) coincides (in new coordinates) with
 ( g 0 , k 0 ) (g_{0},k_{0}) on the ball of radius 1/2. 
 
 • 
 
 ( g , k ) (g,k) is
exactly the Schwarzschild data ( g s x , 0 ) (g^{x}_{s},0) outside the ball of
radius one. 
 
 • 
 
 Moreover, the new data still obeys the smallness
condition ( 4.3 ). 
 
 
 The constructed metric is already in wave coordinates on its
Schwarzschild part. We now describe the procedure which produces
the initial data ( g , ∂ t g ) (g,\partial_{t}g) associated with ( g , k ) (g,k) and
satisfying the wave coordinate condition. 
 
 
 Recall that a priori we are only given the spatial part of
the metric g i ​ j g_{ij} together with a second fundamental form
 k i ​ j k_{ij} . We now define the full space-time metric g α ​ β g_{\alpha\beta} on the
Cauchy hypersurface Σ 0 \Sigma_{0} as follows: 
 

 
 (4.8) 
 
 g 0 ​ i = 0 , g 00 = − a ⁡ ( r ) , g_{0i}=0,\qquad g_{00}=-a(r), 
 
 
 where the function 
 

 
 
 
 a ⁡ ( r ) = r − 2 ​ M r + 2 ​ M , for ​ r > 1 \displaystyle a(r)=\frac{r-2M}{r+2M},\qquad{\text{for}}\,\,\,r>1 
 
 
 
 
 a ⁡ ( r ) = 1 , for ​ r ≤ 1 2 \displaystyle a(r)=1,\qquad{\text{for}}\,\,\,r\leq\frac{1}{2} 
 
 
 Thus defined metric coincides with the full Schwarzschild
metric g s g_{s} for r > 1 r>1 .
We further define 
 

 
 (4.9) 
 
 ∂ t g i ​ j = − 2 ​ a ​ k i ​ j \partial_{t}g_{ij}=-2ak_{ij} 
 
 
 It remains to determine ∂ t g 0 ​ α \partial_{t}g_{0\alpha} . We find it by satisfying
the wave coordinate condition 
 

 
 
 g β ​ μ ​ ∂ μ g α ​ β = 1 2 ​ g μ ​ ν ​ ∂ α g μ ​ ν g^{\beta\mu}\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\mu\nu} 
 
 
 Setting α = 0 \alpha=0 we obtain 
 

 
 
 1 2 g 00 ∂ t g 00 = − g β ​ i ∂ i g 0 ​ β + 1 2 g i ​ j ∂ t g i ​ j \frac{1}{2}g^{00}\partial_{t}g_{00}=-g^{\beta i}\partial_{i}g_{0\beta}+\frac{1}{2}g^{{ij}}\partial_{t}g_{ij} 
 
 
 This defines ∂ t g 00 \partial_{t}g_{00} . On the other hand setting α = i \alpha=i we
obtain 
 

 
 
 g 00 ∂ t g 0 ​ i = − g β ​ j ∂ j g i ​ β + 1 2 g μ ​ ν ∂ i g μ ​ ν g^{00}\partial_{t}g_{0i}=-g^{\beta j}\partial_{j}g_{i\beta}+\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{i}g_{\mu\nu} 
 
 
 This determines ∂ t g 0 ​ i \partial_{t}g_{0i} .
Observe that since the metric g g coincides with the Schwarzschild
metric g s g_{s} , already satisfying the wave coordinate condition,
outside the ball of radius one, we have that on that set the initial data takes
the form ( g s , 0 ) (g_{s},0) .
Hence we constructed the initial data ( g , ∂ t g ) (g,\partial_{t}g) with the
properties that 
 
 • 
 
 The initial data ( g , ∂ t g ) (g,\partial_{t}g) corresponds to the initial
data ( g , k ) (g,k) prescribed originally. 
 
 • 
 
 ( g , ∂ t g ) (g,\partial_{t}g) is
exactly the Schwarzschild data ( g s , 0 ) (g_{s},0) outside the ball of
radius one. 
 
 • 
 
 The initial data verifies the wave coordinate
condition. 
 
 • 
 
 The initial data satisfies the smallness
condition 
 

 
 (4.10) 
 
 ∑ 0 ≤ | I | ≤ N ( ‖ ∂ x I ( g − m ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( B 1 ) + ∑ 0 ≤ | J | ≤ N − 1 ‖ ∂ x J ∂ t g ‖ L 2 ​ ( B 1 ) ) ≤ ϵ \sum_{0\leq|I|\leq N}\Big(\|\partial_{x}^{I}(g-m)\|_{L^{2}(B_{1})}+\sum_{0\leq|J|\leq N-1}\|\partial^{J}_{x}\partial_{t}g\|_{L^{2}(B_{1})}\Big)\leq\epsilon 
 
 
 
 
 Now with the initial data ( g , ∂ t g ) (g,\partial_{t}g) we solve the reduced Einstein
equations ( 3.6 ). It follows from the proof of Lemma
 3.1 that, in the notation Γ λ = g α ​ β ​ Γ α ​ β λ \Gamma^{\lambda}=g^{\alpha\beta}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta} , the reduced Einstein equations can be written in
the form: 
 

 
 (4.11) 
 
 𝐑 α ​ β − 1 2 ​ ( D α ​ Γ β + D β ​ Γ α ) − Γ σ ​ N α ​ β σ ​ ( g , ∂ g ) = 0 . {\bf{R}}_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}(D_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}+D_{\beta}\Gamma_{\alpha})-\Gamma_{\sigma}N^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}(g,\partial g)=0. 
 
 
 Here D D denotes a covariant derivative with respect to the
space-time metric g g and N α ​ β σ N^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta} are some given functions
depending on g g and ∂ g \partial g .
Observe that the initial data ( g , ∂ t g ) (g,\partial_{t}g) were chosen in such
a way that the wave coordinate condition Γ λ = 0 \Gamma^{\lambda}=0 is
satisfied on the initial hypersurface Σ 0 \Sigma_{0} . We now argue that
this condition is propagated, i.e, the solution of the reduced Einstein
equations ( 4.11 ) obeys Γ λ = 0 \Gamma^{\lambda}=0 on any 
hypersuface Σ t \Sigma_{t} . We would have thus
shown that a solution of the reduced Einstein equations is, in fact,
a solution of the vacuum Einstein equations. 
 
 
 To prove that Γ λ = 0 \Gamma^{\lambda}=0 we differentiate ( 4.11 )
and use the contracted Bianchi identity
 D β ​ 𝐑 α ​ β = 1 2 ​ D α ​ 𝐑 D^{\beta}{\bf{R}}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}D_{\alpha}{\bf{R}} 
 

 
 
 0 \displaystyle 0 
 = 2 ​ ( D β ​ 𝐑 α ​ β − 1 2 ​ D α ​ 𝐑 ) = D β ​ D α ​ Γ β + D β ​ D β ​ Γ α − D α ​ D β ​ Γ β − 2 ​ D β ​ ( Γ σ ​ N α ​ β σ ) − D α ​ ( Γ σ ​ N β σ ​ β ) \displaystyle=2(D^{\beta}{\bf{R}}_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}D_{\alpha}{\bf{R}})=D^{\beta}D_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\beta}+D^{\beta}D_{\beta}\Gamma_{\alpha}-D_{\alpha}D^{\beta}\Gamma_{\beta}-2D^{\beta}(\Gamma_{\sigma}N^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta})-D_{\alpha}(\Gamma_{\sigma}N^{\sigma\beta}_{\beta}) 
 
 
 
 
 = D β ​ D β ​ Γ α + 𝐑 α ​ γ ​ Γ γ − 2 ​ ( D β ​ Γ σ ) ​ N β σ ​ β − ( D α ​ Γ σ ) ​ N β σ ​ β − 2 ​ Γ σ ​ ( D β ​ N α σ ​ β ) − Γ σ ​ ( D α ​ N β σ ​ β ) \displaystyle=D^{\beta}D_{\beta}\Gamma_{\alpha}+{\bf{R}}_{\alpha\gamma}\Gamma^{\gamma}-2(D_{\beta}\Gamma_{\sigma})N^{\sigma\beta}_{\beta}-(D_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\sigma})N^{\sigma\beta}_{\beta}-2\Gamma_{\sigma}(D_{\beta}N^{\sigma\beta}_{\alpha})-\Gamma_{\sigma}(D_{\alpha}N^{\sigma\beta}_{\beta}) 
 
 
 Therefore, Γ λ \Gamma^{\lambda} satisfies a covariant wave equation, on the
background determined by the constructed metric g g , with
the initial condition Γ λ = 0 \Gamma^{\lambda}=0 . It remains to show that
 D t ​ Γ λ = 0 D_{t}\Gamma^{\lambda}=0 on Σ 0 \Sigma_{0} and the conclusion that
 Γ λ ≡ 0 \Gamma^{\lambda}\equiv 0 will follows by the uniqueness result for
wave equation. 
 
 
 We recall that the initial data ( g , k ) (g,k) verifies the constraint
equations ( 4.1 ), ( 4.2 ), which imply
that
on Σ 0 \Sigma_{0} 
 

 
 
 𝐑 T ​ T + 1 2 ​ 𝐑 = 0 , 𝐑 T ​ i = 0 , {\bf{R}}_{TT}+\frac{1}{2}{\bf{R}}=0,\qquad{\bf{R}}_{Ti}=0, 
 
 
 where T = − ( g 00 ) − 1 ∂ t T=-(g_{00})^{-1}\partial_{t} is the unit future oriented normal to
 Σ 0 \Sigma_{0} .
Therefore returning to ( 4.11 ) we obtain that 
 

 
 
 
 0 = 𝐑 00 + 1 2 ​ 𝐑 = − ( g 00 ) − 1 ​ D t ​ Γ 0 + D i ​ Γ i , \displaystyle 0={\bf{R}}_{00}+\frac{1}{2}{\bf{R}}=-(g_{00})^{-1}D_{t}\Gamma_{0}+D^{i}\Gamma_{i}, 
 
 
 
 
 0 = 𝐑 0 ​ i = 1 2 ​ D t ​ Γ i + 1 2 ​ D i ​ Γ 0 \displaystyle 0={\bf{R}}_{0i}=\frac{1}{2}D_{t}\Gamma_{i}+\frac{1}{2}D_{i}\Gamma_{0} 
 
 
 This finishes the proof that Γ λ ≡ 0 \Gamma^{\lambda}\equiv 0 . 
 
 
 We also know that the time-independent Schwarzschild metric
 g s g_{s} is a solution of the Einstein vacuum equation
 𝐑 α ​ β = 0 {\bf{R}}_{\alpha\beta}=0 . Moreover, since g s g_{s} satisfies the wave
coordinate condition it also verifies the reduced Einstein
equations ( 4.11 ). Since the initial data ( g , ∂ t g ) = ( g s , 0 ) (g,\partial_{t}g)=(g_{s},0) outside the ball of radius two, constructed solution
will coincide with the Schwarzschild solution in the exterior of
the null cone developed from the sphere of radius one in
 Σ 0 \Sigma_{0} . 
 
 
 We end the discussion of the initial data by comparing the light
cones of Minkowski and Schwarzschild spaces in the wave coordinates
of the Schwarzschild space. 
 
 
 Lemma 4.1 . 
 
 For an arbitrary R > 2 ​ M R>2M the forward null cone of the metric g s g_{s} , intersecting the time slice t = 0 t=0 
along the sphere of radius R R , is contained in the interior of the
Minkowski cone t − r = R t-r=R . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The null cone intersecting the time slice t = 0 t=0 along the
sphere of radius R R can be realized as the level hypersurface
 u = 0 u=0 of the optical function u u solving the eikonal equation 
 

 
 
 g s α ​ β ​ ∂ α u ​ ∂ β u = 0 g_{s}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u\,\,\partial_{\beta}u=0 
 
 
 with the initial condition that u = 0 u=0 on the sphere of radius R R 
at time t = 0 t=0 . Because of the spherical symmetry of the
Schwarzschild metric g s g_{s} and the initial condition we look for
a spherically symmetric solution u = u ⁡ ( t , r ) u=u(t,r) . The eikonal equation
then reads 
 

 
 
 r + 2 ​ M r − 2 ​ M ​ ( ∂ t u ) 2 = r − 2 ​ M r + 2 ​ M ​ ( ∂ r u ) 2 \frac{r+2M}{r-2M}(\partial_{t}u)^{2}=\frac{r-2M}{r+2M}(\partial_{r}u)^{2} 
 
 
 Let t = γ ⁡ ( r ) t=\gamma(r) be a null geodesic, originating from some point on the sphere of radius
 R R at t = 0 t=0 , such that u ⁡ ( γ ⁡ ( r ) , r ) = 0 u(\gamma(r),r)=0 . Then 
 

 
 
 ∂ t u ​ γ ˙ ​ ( r ) + ∂ r u = 0 \partial_{t}u\dot{\gamma}(r)+\partial_{r}u=0 
 
 
 Substituting this into the eikonal equation we obtain that 
 

 
 
 ( r + 2 ​ M r − 2 ​ M ) 2 = | γ ˙ ​ ( r ) | 2 \Big(\frac{r+2M}{r-2M}\Big)^{2}=|\dot{\gamma}(r)|^{2} 
 
 
 Taking the square root and integrating we obtain that 
 

 
 
 γ ⁡ ( r ) = γ ⁡ ( R ) ± ( r − R + 4 ​ M ​ ln ⁡ r − 2 ​ M R − 2 ​ M ) \gamma(r)=\gamma(R)\pm\Big(r-R+4M\ln\frac{r-2M}{R-2M}\Big) 
 
 
 Thus the null geodesics are described the curves 
 

 
 
 t = ± ( r − R + 4 ​ M ​ ln ⁡ r − 2 ​ M R − 2 ​ M ) t=\pm\Big(r-R+4M\ln\frac{r-2M}{R-2M}\Big) 
 
 
 In particular, the forward null cone is contained in the
interior of the set t ≥ r − R t\geq r-R .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 5 The null-frame and null-forms 

 
 Below we introduce a standard Minkowski null-frame used throughout the paper.
At each point ( t , x ) (t,x) we introduce a pair of null vectors
 ( L , L ¯ ) (L,\underline{L}) 
 

 
 
 L 0 = 1 , L i = x i / | x | , i = 1 , 2 , 3 , and L ¯ 0 = 1 , L ¯ i = − x i / | x | , i = 1 , 2 , 3 , L^{0}=1,\quad L^{i}=x^{i}/|x|,\,\,\,\,i=1,2,3,\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{L}^{0}=1,\quad\underline{L}^{i}=-x^{i}/|x|,\,\,\,\,i=1,2,3, 
 
 
 Adding two orthonormal tangent to the sphere S 2 S^{2} vectors
 S 1 , S 2 S_{1},S_{2} which are orthogonal to ω \omega defines a null
frame ( L , L ¯ , S 1 , S 2 ) (L,\underline{L},S_{1},S_{2}) . 
 
 
 Remark 5.1 . 
 
 Since S 2 S^{2} does not admit a global
orthonormal frame S 1 , S 2 S_{1},S_{2} we could alternatively introduce
a global frame induced by the projections of
the coordinate vector fields e i e_{i} . 
 
 
 Let P P be the orthogonal projection of a vector field in ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} 
along ω = x / | x | \omega=x/|x| onto the tangent space of the sphere;
 P ​ V = V − ⟨ V , ω ⟩ ​ ω . PV=V-\langle V,\omega\rangle\omega. 
For i = 1 , 2 , 3 i\!=\!1,2,3 denote the projection of ∂ i \partial_{i} by 
 

 
 (5.1) 
 
 ∂ /  i = A i j ∂ j = ∂ i − ω i ω j ∂ j , where A i j = ( P e i ) j = δ i j − ω i ω j , i = 1 , 2 , 3 , \text{$\partial\mkern-9.0mu$/\penalty\,}_{i}\!=A_{i}^{j}\partial_{j}=\partial_{i}-\omega_{i}\omega^{j}\partial_{j},\qquad\text{where}\qquad A_{i}^{j}=(Pe_{i})^{j}=\delta_{i}^{j}-\omega_{i}\omega^{j},\qquad i=1,2,3, 
 
 
 where e i e_{i} is the usual orthonormal basis in ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} , and
the sums are over j = 1 , 2 , 3 j=1,2,3 only. Let ∂ ¯ 0 = L α ∂ α \bar{\partial}_{0}\!=\!L^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha} and ∂ ¯ i = ∂ /  i \bar{\partial}_{i}=\text{$\partial\mkern-9.0mu$/\penalty\,}_{i} , for i = 1 , 2 , 3 i=1,2,3 . Then
a linear combination of the derivatives
 { ∂ ¯ 0 , … , ∂ ¯ 3 } \{\bar{\partial}_{0},...,\bar{\partial}_{3}\} spans the tangent space of the forward
light cone. 
 
 
 
 In what follows A , B A,B will denote any of the vectorfields S 1 , S 2 S_{1},S_{2} .
We will use the summation conventions 
 

 
 
 X A ​ A α = X β ​ S 1 β ​ S 1 α + X β ​ S 2 β ​ S 2 α , X A ​ Y A = X α ​ Y β ​ S 1 α ​ S 1 β + X α ​ Y β ​ S 2 α ​ S 2 β . X^{A}A^{\alpha}=X^{\beta}{S_{1}}_{\beta}S_{1}^{\alpha}+X^{\beta}{S_{2}}_{\beta}S_{2}^{\alpha},\qquad X_{A}Y_{A}=X^{\alpha}Y^{\beta}{S_{1}}_{\alpha}{S_{1}}_{\beta}+X^{\alpha}Y^{\beta}{S_{2}}_{\alpha}{S_{2}}_{\beta}. 
 
 
 Obvious generalizations of the above
conventions will be used for higher order tensors. 
 
 
 We record the following null frame decomposition of a vector field X = X α ∂ α X=X^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha} :
 X α = X L ​ L α + X L ¯ ​ L ¯ α + X A ​ A α X^{\alpha}=X^{L}L^{\alpha}+X^{\underline{L}}\underline{L}^{\alpha}+X^{A}A^{\alpha} .
Relative to a
null frame the Minkowski metric m m has the following form 
 

 
 
 m L ​ L = m L ¯ ​ L ¯ = m L ​ A = m L ¯ ​ A = 0 , m L ​ L ¯ = m L ¯ ​ L = − 2 , m A ​ B = δ A ​ B , m_{LL}=m_{\underline{L}\underline{L}}=m_{LA}=m_{\underline{L}A}=0,\qquad m_{L\underline{L}}=m_{\underline{L}L}=-2,\qquad m_{AB}=\delta_{AB}, 
 
 
 i.e.
 m α ​ β ​ X α ​ Y β = − 2 ​ ( X L ​ Y L ¯ + X L ¯ ​ Y L ) + X A ​ Y A m_{\alpha\beta}X^{\alpha}Y^{\beta}=-2(X^{L}Y^{\underline{L}}+X^{\underline{L}}Y^{L})+X^{A}Y^{A} .
Recall that we raise and lower indices of any tensor relative
to the Minkowski metric m m , i.e., X α = m α ​ β ​ X β X_{\alpha}=m_{\alpha\beta}X^{\beta} .
We define X Y = m α ​ β ​ X α ​ Y β = X α ​ Y α X_{Y}=m_{\alpha\beta}X^{\alpha}Y^{\beta}=X_{\alpha}Y^{\alpha} .
Then X Y = X L ​ Y L + X L ¯ ​ Y L ¯ + X A ​ Y A X_{Y}=X^{L}Y_{L}+X^{\underline{L}}Y_{\underline{L}}+X^{A}Y_{A} .
It is useful to remember the following rule: 
 

 
 
 X L = − 1 2 ​ X L ¯ , X L ¯ = − 1 2 ​ X L , X A = X A . X^{L}=-\frac{1}{2}X_{\underline{L}},\quad X^{\underline{L}}=-\frac{1}{2}X_{L},\quad X^{A}=X_{A}. 
 
 
 Then 
 

 
 
 m L ​ L = m L ¯ ​ L ¯ = m L ​ A = m L ¯ ​ A = 0 , m L ​ L ¯ = m L ¯ ​ L = − 1 / 2 , m A ​ B = δ A ​ B m^{LL}=m^{\underline{L}\underline{L}}=m^{LA}=m^{\underline{L}A}=0,\qquad m^{L\underline{L}}=m^{\underline{L}L}=-1/2,\qquad m^{AB}=\delta^{AB} 
 
 
 i.e. m α ​ β ​ X α ​ Y β = − 1 2 ​ ( X L ​ Y L ¯ + X L ¯ ​ Y L ) + X A ​ Y A m^{\alpha\beta}X_{\alpha}Y_{\beta}=-\frac{1}{2}\big(X_{L}Y_{\underline{L}}+X_{\underline{L}}Y_{L}\big)+X_{A}Y_{A} . 
 
 
 Definition 5.2 . 
 
 Denote q = r − t q=r-t and s = t + r s=t+r the null coordinates of the Minkowski metric m m 
and ∂ q = 1 2 ( ∂ r − ∂ t ) \partial_{q}=\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{r}-\partial_{t}) 
and ∂ s = 1 2 ( ∂ t + ∂ r ) \partial_{s}=\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r}) , the corresponding null vector fields 
 
 
 
 Let
 k X ​ Y = k α ​ β ​ X α ​ Y β k_{XY}=k_{\alpha\beta}X^{\alpha}Y^{\beta} .
Then 
 

 
 (5.2) 
 
 tr ⁡ k = m α ​ β ​ k α ​ β = − 1 2 ​ ( k L ​ L ¯ + k L ¯ ​ L ) + tr ¯ ​ k \operatorname{tr}\,k=m^{\alpha\beta}k_{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{1}{2}\big(k_{L\underline{L}}+k_{\underline{L}L}\big)+\overline{\operatorname{tr}}\,k 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 (5.3) 
 
 tr ¯ ​ k = δ A ​ B ​ k A ​ B = δ ¯ i ​ j ​ k i ​ j , and δ ¯ i ​ j = δ i ​ j − ω i ​ ω j \overline{\operatorname{tr}}\,k=\delta^{AB}k_{AB}=\overline{\delta}^{ij}k_{ij},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{\delta}^{ij}=\delta^{ij}-\omega^{i}\omega^{j} 
 
 
 where the sum is over i , j = 1 , 2 , 3 i,j=1,2,3 only. 
 
 
 If k k and p p are symmetric it follows that 
 

 
 (5.4) 
 
 p α ​ β ​ k α ​ β = m α ​ α ′ ​ m β ​ β ′ ​ p α ​ β ​ k α ′ ​ β ′ = 1 4 ​ ( p L ​ L ​ k L ¯ ​ L ¯ + p L ¯ ​ L ¯ ​ k L ​ L + 2 ​ p L ​ L ¯ ​ k L ¯ ​ L ) − δ A ​ B ​ ( p A ​ L ​ k B ​ L ¯ + p A ​ L ¯ ​ k B ​ L ) + δ A ​ B ​ δ A ′ ​ B ′ ​ p A ​ A ′ ​ k B ​ B ′ = 1 4 ​ ( p L ​ L ​ k L ¯ ​ L ¯ + p L ¯ ​ L ¯ ​ k L ​ L + 2 ​ p L ​ L ¯ ​ k L ¯ ​ L ) − δ ¯ i ​ j ​ ( p i ​ L ​ k j ​ L ¯ + p i ​ L ¯ ​ k j ​ L ) + δ ¯ i ​ j ​ δ ¯ i ′ ​ j ′ ​ p i ​ i ′ ​ k j ​ j ′ p_{\alpha\beta}k^{\alpha\beta}=m^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}m^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}p_{\alpha\beta}k_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\\
=\frac{1}{4}\big(p_{LL}k_{\underline{L}\underline{L}}+p_{\underline{L}\underline{L}}k_{{L}{L}}+2p_{L\underline{L}}k_{\underline{L}L}\big)-\delta^{AB}\big(p_{AL}k_{B\underline{L}}+p_{A\underline{L}}k_{B{L}}\big)+\delta^{AB}\delta^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}p_{AA^{\prime}}k_{BB^{\prime}}\\

 Lemma 5.3 . 
 
 With P ⁡ ( p , k ) P(p,k) given by ( 3.20 ) we have for symmetric 2-tensors p p and k k : 
 

 
 (5.5) 
 
 P ⁡ ( p , k ) = 1 4 ​ m α ​ β ​ p α ​ β ​ m α ​ β ​ k α ​ β − 1 2 ​ m α ​ α ′ ​ m β ​ β ′ ​ p α ​ β ​ k α ′ ​ β ′ = − 1 8 ​ ( p L ​ L ​ k L ¯ ​ L ¯ + p L ¯ ​ L ¯ ​ k L ​ L ) − 1 4 ​ δ A ​ B ​ δ A ′ ​ B ′ ​ ( 2 ​ p A ​ A ′ ​ k B ​ B ′ − p A ​ B ​ k A ′ ​ B ′ ) + 1 4 ​ δ A ​ B ​ ( 2 ​ p A ​ L ​ k B ​ L ¯ + 2 ​ p A ​ L ¯ ​ k B ​ L − p A ​ B ​ k L ​ L ¯ − p L ​ L ¯ ​ k A ​ B ) P(p,k)=\frac{1}{4}m^{\alpha\beta}p_{\alpha\beta}m^{\alpha\beta}k_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}m^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}m^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}p_{\alpha\beta}k_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\\
=-\frac{1}{8}\big(p_{LL}k_{\underline{L}\underline{L}}+p_{\underline{L}\underline{L}}k_{{L}{L}}\big)-\frac{1}{4}\delta^{AB}\delta^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\Big(2p_{AA^{\prime}}k_{BB^{\prime}}-p_{AB}k_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\Big)\\
+\frac{1}{4}\delta^{AB}\big(2p_{AL}k_{B\underline{L}}+2p_{A\underline{L}}k_{B{L}}-p_{AB}k_{L\underline{L}}-p_{L\underline{L}}k_{AB}\big) 
 
 
 i.e. at least one of the factors contains only tangential components. 
 
 
 
 Furthermore 
 

 
 
 p α ​ β ∂ α = p L ​ β ∂ L + p L ¯ ​ β ∂ L ¯ + p A ​ β ∂ A = − 1 2 p L ¯ β ∂ L − 1 2 p L β ∂ L ¯ + p A ​ β ∂ A p^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}=p^{L\beta}\partial_{L}+p^{\underline{L}\beta}\partial_{\underline{L}}+p^{A\beta}\partial_{A}=-\frac{1}{2}p_{\underline{L}}^{\,\,\,\beta}\partial_{L}-\frac{1}{2}p_{L}^{\,\,\,\beta}\partial_{\underline{L}}+p^{A\beta}\partial_{A} 
 
 
 
 
 We introduce the following notation. Let 𝒯 = { L , S 1 , S 2 } {\cal T}=\{L,S_{1},S_{2}\} ,
 𝒰 = { L ¯ , L , S 1 , S 2 } {\cal U}=\{\underline{L},L,S_{1},S_{2}\} , ℒ = { L } {\cal L}=\{L\} and 𝒮 = { S 1 , S 2 } {\cal S}=\{S_{1},S_{2}\} .
For any two of these families 𝒱 {\cal V} and 𝒲 {\cal W} and an
arbitrary two-tensor p p we denote 
 

 
 (5.6) 
 
 | p | 𝒱𝒲 \displaystyle|p|_{{\cal VW}} 
 = ∑ V ∈ 𝒱 , W ∈ 𝒲 , | p β ​ γ V β W γ | , \displaystyle=\sum_{V\in{\cal V},W\in{\cal W},}|p_{\beta\gamma}V^{\beta}W^{\gamma}|,

 Let Q Q denote a null form, i.e. Q α ​ β ​ ( ∂ ϕ , ∂ ψ ) = ∂ α ϕ ​ ∂ β ψ − ∂ β ϕ ​ ∂ α ψ Q_{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi,\partial\psi)=\partial_{\alpha}\phi\,\partial_{\beta}\psi-\partial_{\beta}\phi\,\partial_{\alpha}\psi 
if α ≠ β \alpha\neq\beta and Q 0 ​ ( ∂ ϕ , ∂ ψ ) = m α ​ β ​ ∂ α ϕ ​ ∂ β ψ Q_{0}(\partial\phi,\partial\psi)=m^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\phi\,\partial_{\beta}\psi . 
 
 
 Lemma 5.4 . 
 
 If P P is as in Lemma
 5.3 then 
 

 
 (5.9) 
 
 | P ⁡ ( p , k ) | ≲ | p | 𝒯𝒰 ​ | k | 𝒯𝒰 + | p | L ​ L ​ | k | + | ​ p | | k | L ​ L |P(p,k)|\lesssim|p\,|_{\cal TU}|k|_{\cal TU}+|p\,|_{LL}|k|+|p\,|\,|k|_{LL} 
 
 
 If Q ⁡ ( ∂ ϕ , ∂ ψ ) Q(\partial\phi,\partial\psi) is a null form then 
 

 
 (5.10) 
 
 | Q ⁡ ( ∂ ϕ , ∂ ψ ) | ≲ | ∂ ¯ ​ ϕ | ​ | ∂ ϕ | + | ∂ ϕ | ​ | ∂ ¯ ​ ψ | |Q(\partial\phi,\partial\psi)|\lesssim|\overline{\partial}\phi||\partial\phi|+|\partial\phi||\overline{\partial}\psi| 
 
 
 
 
 Furthermore 
 

 
 (5.11) 
 
 | k α ​ β ​ ∂ α ϕ ​ ∂ β ϕ | ≲ ( | k | L ​ L ​ | ∂ ϕ | 2 + | k ​ ‖ ∂ ¯ ​ ϕ ‖ ​ ∂ ϕ | ) |k^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\phi\,\partial_{\beta}\phi|\lesssim\big(|k|_{LL}|\partial\phi|^{2}+|k|\,|\bar{\partial}\phi||\partial\phi|\big) 
 
 
 

 
 (5.12) 
 
 | L α ​ k α ​ β ​ ∂ β ϕ | ≲ ( | k | L ​ L ​ | ∂ ϕ | + | k | ​ | ∂ ¯ ​ ϕ | ) |L_{\alpha}k^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi|\lesssim\big(|k|_{LL}|\partial\phi|+|k|\,|\bar{\partial}\phi|\big) 
 
 
 

 
 (5.13) 
 
 | ( ∂ α k α ​ β ) ​ ∂ β ϕ | ≲ ( | ∂ k | L ​ L + | ∂ ¯ ​ k | ) ​ | ∂ ϕ | + | ∂ k | ​ | ∂ ¯ ​ ϕ | |(\partial_{\alpha}k^{\alpha\beta})\partial_{\beta}\phi|\lesssim\big(|\partial k|_{LL}+|\overline{\partial}k|\big)\,|\partial\phi|+|\partial k|\,|\overline{\partial}\phi| 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof of ( 5.10 ) for the null form Q 0 Q_{0} 
follows directly from ( 5.2 ). To prove the claim for the
null forms Q α ​ β Q_{\alpha\beta} use that 
 

 
 (5.14) 
 
 ∂ i = L i ( ∂ s + ∂ q ) + ∂ ¯ i , i = 1 , 2 , 3 , ∂ 0 = L 0 ( ∂ s − ∂ q ) \partial_{i}=L_{i}(\partial_{s}+\partial_{q})+\bar{\partial}_{i},\quad i=1,2,3,\qquad\partial_{0}=L_{0}(\partial_{s}-\partial_{q}) 
 
 
 Therefore, 
 

 
 
 | Q α ​ β ​ ( ∂ ϕ , ∂ ψ ) | = | ∂ α ϕ ​ ∂ β ψ − ∂ β ϕ ​ ∂ α ψ | ≤ C ​ | ∂ ¯ ​ ϕ ​ ‖ ∂ ψ ​ | + C | ​ ∂ ϕ ‖ ​ ∂ ¯ ​ ψ | |Q_{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi,\partial\psi)|=|\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial_{\beta}\psi-\partial_{\beta}\phi\partial_{\alpha}\psi|\leq C|\bar{\partial}\phi|\,|\partial\psi|+C|\partial\phi|\,|\bar{\partial}\psi| 
 
 
 
 
 The estimates
( 5.11 )-( 5.13 ) follow from
( 5.4 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma 5.5 . 
 
 If k α ​ β k^{\alpha\beta} is a symmetric tensor
and ϕ \phi a function then 
 

 
 (5.15) 
 
 | k α ​ β ​ ∂ α ∂ β ϕ | ≲ ( | k | L ​ L ​ | ∂ 2 ϕ | + | k | ​ | ∂ ¯ ​ ∂ ϕ | ) |k^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\phi|\lesssim\big(|k|_{LL}|\partial^{2}\phi|+|k|\,|\bar{\partial}\partial\phi|\big) 
 
 
 Also,
with tr ¯ ​ k = δ A ​ B ​ k A ​ B = ( δ i ​ j − ω i ​ ω j ) ​ k i ​ j \overline{\operatorname{tr}}\,k=\delta^{AB}k_{AB}=(\delta^{ij}-\omega^{i}\omega^{j})k_{ij} we have 
 

 
 (5.16) 
 
 | k α ​ β ​ ∂ α ∂ β ϕ − k L ​ L ​ ∂ q 2 ϕ − 2 ​ k L ​ L ¯ ​ ∂ s ∂ q ϕ − r − 1 ​ tr ¯ ​ k ​ ∂ q ϕ | ≲ | k | L ​ 𝒯 | ∂ ¯ ​ ∂ ϕ | + | k | ( | ∂ ¯ 2 ​ ϕ | + r − 1 ​ | ∂ ¯ ​ ϕ | ) . \big|k^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\phi-k_{LL}\partial_{q}^{2}\phi-2k_{L\underline{L}}\partial_{s}\partial_{q}\phi-r^{-1}\,\overline{\operatorname{tr}}\,k\,\partial_{q}\phi\big|\lesssim|k|_{L{\cal T}}|\bar{\partial}\partial\phi|+|k|\,\big(|\bar{\partial}^{2}\phi|+r^{-1}|\bar{\partial}\phi|\big). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The estimate ( 5.15 ) follow from
( 5.4 ).
We have 
 

 
 (5.17) 
 
 ∂ i ω j = r − 1 ​ ( δ i ​ j − ω i ​ ω j ) = r − 1 ​ δ ¯ i ​ j \partial_{i}\omega_{j}=r^{-1}(\delta_{ij}-\omega_{i}\omega_{j})=r^{-1}\overline{\delta}_{ij} 
 
 
 Furthermore ∂ i = ∂ ¯ i + ω i ∂ r \partial_{i}=\bar{\partial}_{i}+\omega_{i}\partial_{r} ,
where ∂ r = ω j ∂ j \partial_{r}=\omega^{j}\partial_{j} so [ ∂ ¯ i , ∂ r ] = ( ∂ ¯ i ω k ) ∂ k [\bar{\partial}_{i},\partial_{r}]=(\bar{\partial}_{i}\omega^{k})\partial_{k} and 
 

 
 (5.18) 
 
 ∂ i ∂ j = ( ∂ ¯ i + ω i ∂ r ) ( ∂ ¯ j + ω j ∂ r ) = ∂ ¯ i ∂ ¯ j + ω i ω k ∂ ¯ j ∂ k + ω j ω k ∂ ¯ i ∂ k + ω i ω j ∂ r 2 + ( ∂ ¯ i ω j ) ∂ r + ω j ( ∂ ¯ i ω k ) ∂ k = ∂ ¯ i ∂ ¯ j + ω i ∂ ¯ j ∂ r + ω j ∂ ¯ i ∂ r + ω i ω j ∂ r 2 + r − 1 δ ¯ i ​ j ∂ r − r − 1 ω i ∂ ¯ j \partial_{i}\partial_{j}=(\bar{\partial}_{i}+\omega_{i}\partial_{r})(\bar{\partial}_{j}+\omega_{j}\partial_{r})\\
=\bar{\partial}_{i}\bar{\partial}_{j}+\omega_{i}\,\omega^{k}\,\bar{\partial}_{j}\partial_{k}+\omega_{j}\omega^{k}\,\bar{\partial}_{i}\partial_{k}+\omega_{i}\omega_{j}\partial_{r}^{2}+(\bar{\partial}_{i}\omega_{j})\partial_{r}+\omega_{j}(\bar{\partial}_{i}\omega^{k})\partial_{k}\\
=\bar{\partial}_{i}\bar{\partial}_{j}+\omega_{i}\,\bar{\partial}_{j}\partial_{r}+\omega_{j}\,\bar{\partial}_{i}\partial_{r}+\omega_{i}\omega_{j}\partial_{r}^{2}+r^{-1}\overline{\delta}_{ij}\partial_{r}-r^{-1}\omega_{i}\bar{\partial}_{j} 
 
 
 Furthermore 
 

 
 (5.19) 
 
 ∂ 0 ∂ i = ∂ t ( ∂ ¯ i + ω i ∂ r ) = ∂ ¯ i ∂ t + ω i ∂ t ∂ r \partial_{0}\partial_{i}=\partial_{t}(\bar{\partial}_{i}+\omega_{i}\partial_{r})=\bar{\partial}_{i}\partial_{t}+\omega_{i}\partial_{t}\partial_{r} 
 
 
 Hence 
 

 
 (5.20) 
 
 k α ​ β ∂ α ∂ β = k 00 ∂ t 2 + 2 k 0 ​ i ω i ∂ t ∂ r + k i ​ j ω i ω j ∂ r 2 + r − 1 tr ¯ k ∂ r + k i ​ j ∂ ¯ i ∂ ¯ j − r − 1 k i ​ j ω i ∂ ¯ j + 2 k 0 ​ j ∂ ¯ j ∂ t + 2 k i ​ j ω i ∂ ¯ j ∂ r k^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}=k^{00}\partial_{t}^{2}+2k^{0i}\omega_{i}\partial_{t}\partial_{r}+k^{ij}\omega_{i}\omega_{j}\partial_{r}^{2}+r^{-1}\overline{\operatorname{tr}}\,k\,\partial_{r}\\
+k^{ij}\bar{\partial}_{i}\bar{\partial}_{j}-r^{-1}k^{ij}\omega_{i}\bar{\partial}_{j}+2k^{0j}\bar{\partial}_{j}\partial_{t}+2k^{ij}\omega_{i}\bar{\partial}_{j}\partial_{r} 
 
 
 If we substitute ∂ t = ∂ s − ∂ q \partial_{t}=\partial_{s}-\partial_{q} , ∂ r = ∂ s + ∂ q \partial_{r}=\partial_{s}+\partial_{q} 
and identify 
 

 
 (5.21) 
 
 k L ​ L = k 00 − 2 ​ k 0 ​ i ​ ω i + k i ​ j ​ ω i ​ ω j , k L ​ L ¯ = − k 00 + k i ​ j ​ ω i ​ ω j , k L ¯ ​ L ¯ = k 00 + 2 ​ k 0 ​ i ​ ω i + k i ​ j ​ ω i ​ ω j k_{LL}=k^{00}-2k^{0i}\omega_{i}+k^{ij}\omega_{i}\omega_{j},\qquad k_{L\underline{L}}=-k^{00}+k^{ij}\omega_{i}\omega_{j},\qquad k_{\underline{L}\underline{L}}=k^{00}+2k^{0i}\omega_{i}+k^{ij}\omega_{i}\omega_{j} 
 
 
 and 
 

 
 (5.22) 
 
 − k 0 ​ j + k i ​ j ​ ω j = k 0 j + k i j ​ ω i = k L j , k 0 ​ j + k i ​ j ​ ω j = − k 0 j + k i j ​ ω i = k L ¯ j -k^{0j}+k^{ij}\omega_{j}=k_{0}^{\,\,\,j}+k_{i}^{\,\,\,j}\omega^{i}=k_{L}^{\,\,\,j},\qquad k^{0j}+k^{ij}\omega_{j}=-k_{0}^{\,\,\,j}+k_{i}^{\,\,\,j}\omega^{i}=k_{\underline{L}}^{\,\,\,j} 
 
 
 we get 
 

 
 (5.23) 
 
 k α ​ β ∂ α ∂ β = k L ​ L ∂ q 2 + 2 k L ​ L ¯ ∂ s ∂ q + k L ¯ ​ L ¯ ∂ s 2 + r − 1 tr ¯ k ∂ q + k i ​ j ∂ ¯ i ∂ ¯ j + r − 1 tr ¯ k ∂ s − r − 1 k i ​ j ω i ∂ ¯ j + 2 k L j ∂ ¯ j ∂ q + 2 k L ¯ j ∂ ¯ j ∂ s k^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}=k_{LL}\partial_{q}^{2}+2k_{L\underline{L}}\partial_{s}\partial_{q}+k_{\underline{L}\underline{L}}\partial_{s}^{2}+r^{-1}\overline{\operatorname{tr}}\,k\,\partial_{q}\\
+k^{ij}\bar{\partial}_{i}\bar{\partial}_{j}+r^{-1}\overline{\operatorname{tr}}\,k\,\partial_{s}-r^{-1}k^{ij}\omega_{i}\bar{\partial}_{j}+2k_{L}^{\,\,\,j}\bar{\partial}_{j}\partial_{q}+2k_{\underline{L}}^{\,\,\,j}\bar{\partial}_{j}\partial_{s} 
 
 
 Finally, we can also write 
 

 
 (5.24) 
 
 2 k L j ∂ ¯ j ∂ q = k L j ∂ ¯ j ( ω k ∂ k − ∂ t ) = k L j ω k ∂ ¯ j ∂ k − k L j ∂ ¯ j ∂ t + r − 1 k L j ∂ ¯ j , 2k_{L}^{\,\,\,j}\bar{\partial}_{j}\partial_{q}=k_{L}^{\,\,\,j}\bar{\partial}_{j}(\omega^{k}\partial_{k}-\partial_{t})=k_{L}^{\,\,\,j}\omega^{k}\bar{\partial}_{j}\partial_{k}-k_{L}^{\,\,\,j}\bar{\partial}_{j}\partial_{t}+r^{-1}k_{L}^{\,\,\,j}\bar{\partial}_{j}, 
 
 
 since ( ∂ ¯ j ω k ) ∂ k = r − 1 ∂ ¯ j (\bar{\partial}_{j}\omega^{k})\partial_{k}=r^{-1}\bar{\partial}_{j} .
The inequality ( 5.16 ) now follows.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary 5.6 . 
 
 Let ϕ \phi be a solution of the reduced wave equation □ ~ g ​ ϕ = F \widetilde{\square}_{g}\phi=F with a metric g g such that H α ​ β = g α ​ β − m α ​ β H^{\alpha\beta}=g^{\alpha\beta}-m^{\alpha\beta} 
satisfies the condition that | H L ​ L ¯ | < 1 4 |H^{L\underline{L}}|<\frac{1}{4} . Then 
 

 
 (5.25) 
 
 | ( 4 ∂ s − H L ​ L 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ ∂ q − tr ¯ ​ H + H L ​ L ¯ 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ ​ r ) ∂ q ( r ϕ ) + r ​ F 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ | ≲ r | △ ω ϕ | + | H | L ​ 𝒯 r | ∂ ¯ ∂ ϕ | + | H | ( r | ∂ ¯ 2 ϕ | + | ∂ ¯ ϕ | + r − 1 | ϕ | ) \Big|\Big(4\partial_{s}-\frac{H_{LL}}{2g^{L\underline{L}}}\partial_{q}-\frac{\overline{\operatorname{tr}}\,H+H_{L\underline{L}}}{2g^{L\underline{L}}\,\,r}\Big)\partial_{q}(r\phi)+\frac{rF}{2g^{L\underline{L}}}\Big|\lesssim r|\triangle_{\omega}\phi|+|H|_{L{\cal T}}\,\,r\,|\bar{\partial}\partial\phi|+|H|\,\big(r\,|\bar{\partial}^{2}\phi|+|\bar{\partial}\phi|+r^{-1}|\phi|\big) 
 
 
 where
 △ ω = △ ¯ = δ i ​ j ​ ∂ ¯ i ​ ∂ ¯ j \triangle_{\omega}=\bar{\triangle}=\delta^{ij}\bar{\partial}_{i}\bar{\partial}_{j} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Define the new metric 
 

 
 
 g ~ α ​ β = g α ​ β − 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ . \tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta}=\frac{g^{\alpha\beta}}{-2g^{L\underline{L}}}. 
 
 
 The equation g α ​ β ​ ∂ α ∂ β ϕ = F g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\phi=F then takes the form 
 

 
 
 g ~ α ​ β ​ ∂ α ∂ β ϕ = F − 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ , \tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\phi=\frac{F}{-2g^{L\underline{L}}}, 
 
 
 which can also be written as 
 

 
 
 □ ​ ϕ + ( g ~ α ​ β − m α ​ β ) ​ ∂ α ∂ β ϕ = F − 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ \square\phi+(\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta}-m^{\alpha\beta})\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\phi=\frac{F}{-2g^{L\underline{L}}} 
 
 
 Let k α ​ β k^{\alpha\beta} be the tensor k α ​ β = ( g ~ α ​ β − m α ​ β ) k^{\alpha\beta}=(\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta}-m^{\alpha\beta}) 
Observe that 
 

 
 
 k α ​ β = ( − 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ ) − 1 ​ ( g α ​ β + 2 ​ m α ​ β ​ g L ​ L ¯ ) \displaystyle k^{\alpha\beta}=(-2g^{L\underline{L}})^{-1}\big(g^{\alpha\beta}+2m^{\alpha\beta}g^{L\underline{L}}\big) 
 = ( − 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ ) − 1 ​ ( H α ​ β + m α ​ β ​ ( 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ + 1 ) ) \displaystyle=(-2g^{L\underline{L}})^{-1}\big(H^{\alpha\beta}+m^{\alpha\beta}(2g^{L\underline{L}}+1)\big) 
 
 
 
 
 = ( − 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ ) − 1 ​ ( H α ​ β + 2 ​ m α ​ β ​ H L ​ L ¯ ) \displaystyle=(-2g^{L\underline{L}})^{-1}\big(H^{\alpha\beta}+2m^{\alpha\beta}H^{L\underline{L}}\big) 
 
 
 Thus, 
 

 
 (5.26) 
 
 k L ​ L ¯ = 0 , k L ​ 𝒯 = ( − 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ ) − 1 ​ H L ​ 𝒯 , tr ¯ ​ k = ( − 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ ) − 1 ​ ( tr ¯ ​ H + H L ​ L ¯ ) k_{L\underline{L}}=0,\qquad\quad k_{L{\cal T}}=(-2g^{L\underline{L}})^{-1}H_{L{\cal T}},\qquad\quad\overline{\operatorname{tr}}\,k\,=(-2g^{L\underline{L}})^{-1}\big(\overline{\operatorname{tr}}\,H\,+H_{L\underline{L}}\big) 
 
 
 Moreover, | k | ≲ | H | |k|\lesssim|H| , since g L ​ L ¯ = H L ​ L ¯ − 1 2 g^{L\underline{L}}=H^{L\underline{L}}-\frac{1}{2} 
and by the assumptions of the Corollary | H L ​ L ¯ | < 1 4 |H^{L\underline{L}}|<\frac{1}{4} . 
 
 
 Now using ( 5.16 ) of Lemma 5.5 , with the condition that
 k L ​ L ¯ = 0 k_{L\underline{L}}=0 , together with the
decomposition 
 

 
 
 □ ϕ = − ∂ t 2 ϕ + △ ϕ = 1 r ( ∂ t + ∂ r ) ( ∂ r − ∂ t ) r ϕ + △ ω ϕ = 4 r ∂ s ∂ q r ϕ + △ ω ϕ . \square\phi=-\partial_{t}^{2}\phi+\triangle\phi=\frac{1}{r}(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r})(\partial_{r}-\partial_{t})r\phi+\triangle_{\omega}\phi=\frac{4}{r}\partial_{s}\partial_{q}r\phi+\triangle_{\omega}\phi. 
 
 
 we find that the identity
 □ ​ ϕ + k α ​ β ​ ∂ α ∂ β ϕ = ( − 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ ) − 1 ​ F \square\phi+k^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\phi=({-2g^{L\underline{L}}})^{-1}F 
leads to the inequality 
 

 
 
 | 4 ​ ∂ s ∂ q r ​ ϕ + r ​ k L ​ L ​ ∂ q 2 ϕ + tr ¯ ​ k ​ ∂ q ϕ + ( 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ ) − 1 ​ r ​ F | ≲ r ​ | △ ω ​ ϕ | + r ​ | k | L ​ 𝒯 | ∂ ¯ ​ ∂ ϕ | + | k | ( r ​ | ∂ ¯ 2 ​ ϕ | + | ∂ ¯ ​ ϕ | ) \big|4\partial_{s}\partial_{q}r\phi+rk_{LL}\partial_{q}^{2}\phi+\overline{\operatorname{tr}}\,k\,\partial_{q}\phi+(2g^{L\underline{L}})^{-1}rF\big|\lesssim r|\triangle_{\omega}\phi|+r|k|_{L{\cal T}}|\bar{\partial}\partial\phi|+|k|\,\big(r\,|\bar{\partial}^{2}\phi|+|\bar{\partial}\phi|\big) 
 
 
 Finally, identity ( 5.26 ) and a crude estimate | k | ≲ | H | |k|\lesssim|H| yield the desired result.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 6 The weak null condition and
asymptotic expansion of Einstein’s equation’s in wave coordinates 

 
 Let us now first describe the weak null condition. The results of this section
appear in [ L-R ] .
Consider the Cauchy problem for a system of nonlinear wave equations in three space dimensions: 
 

 
 (6.1) 
 
 −   
      
      
   u i = F i ( u , u ′ , u ′′ ) , i = 1 , … , N , u = ( u 1 , … , u N ) , -\,\,\hbox{\vrule\vbox to7.0pt{\hrule\hbox to7.0pt{\hfil}\vfil\hrule}\vrule}\,\,u_{i}\,\,=F_{i}(u,u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime}),\qquad i=1,...,N,\qquad u=(u_{1},...,u_{N}), 
 
 
 where − □ = − ∂ t 2 + ∑ j = 1 3 ∂ x j 2 -\square=-\partial_{t}^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\partial_{x^{j}}^{2} .
We assume that F F is a function of u u and its derivatives
of the form 
 

 
 (6.2) 
 
 F i ​ ( u , u ′ , u ′′ ) = a i ​ α ​ β j ​ k ​ ∂ α u j ​ ∂ β u k + G i ​ ( u , u ′ , u ′′ ) , \,\,\,F_{i}(u,u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime})={a_{i\alpha\beta}^{jk}\partial^{\alpha}u_{j}\,\partial^{\beta}u_{k}}+G_{i}(u,u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime}), 
 
 
 where G i ​ ( u , u ′ , u ′′ ) G_{i}(u,u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime}) vanishes to third order as
 ( u , u ′ , u ′′ ) → 0 (u,u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime})\!\to\!0 and
 a i ​ α ​ β j ​ k = 0 a_{i\,\alpha\beta}^{jk}\!=\!0 unless | α | ≤ | β | ≤ |\alpha|\!\leq\!|\beta|\!\leq 2\! and
 | β | ≥ 1 |\beta|\!\geq\!1 .
Here we used the summation convention over repeated indices.
We assume that the initial data 
 

 
 (6.3) 
 
 u ⁡ ( 0 , x ) = ε ​ u 0 ​ ( x ) ∈ C ∞ , u t ​ ( 0 , x ) = ε ​ u 1 ​ ( x ) ∈ C ∞ u(0,x)=\varepsilon u_{0}(x)\in C^{\infty},\quad u_{t}(0,x)=\varepsilon u_{1}(x)\in C^{\infty} 
 
 
 is small and decays fast as | x | → ∞ |x|\to\infty .
We are going to determine conditions on the nonlinearity
such that the equation ( 6.1 ) is
compatible with
the asymptotic expansion as | x | → ∞ |x|\to\infty and | x | ∼ t |x|\sim t 
 

 
 (6.4) 
 
 u ⁡ ( t , x ) ∼ ε ​ U ​ ( q , s , ω ) / | x | ,  where  q = | x | − t , s = ε ​ ln ⁡ | x | , ω = x / | x | , u(t,x)\sim\varepsilon U(q,s,\omega)/|x|,\quad\text{ where }\quad q=|x|-t,\,\,\,s=\varepsilon\ln{|x|},\,\,\,\omega=x/|x|, 
 
 
 for all sufficiently small ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 .
The linear and some nonlinear wave equations allow for such an expansion
with U U independent of s s and the next term
decaying like ε / | x | 2 \varepsilon/|x|^{2} , see [ H1 , H2 ] .
Substituting ( 6.4 ) into ( 6.1 )
and equating powers of order ε 2 / | x | 2 \varepsilon^{2}/|x|^{2} 
we see that 
 

 
 (6.5) 
 
 2 ​ ∂ s ∂ q U i = A i ​ m ​ n j ​ k ​ ( ω ) ​ ( ∂ q m U j ) ​ ( ∂ q n U k ) , U | s = 0 = F 0 , 2\partial_{s}\partial_{q}U_{i}=A_{i\,mn}^{jk}(\omega)(\partial_{q}^{m}U_{j})(\partial_{q}^{n}U_{k}),\qquad\quad U\big|_{s=0}=F_{0}, 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 (6.6) 
 
 A i , m ​ n j ​ k ​ ( ω ) = ∑ | α | = m , | β | = n a i , α ​ β j ​ k ​ ω ^ α ​ ω ^ β , where  ​ ω ^ = ( − 1 , ω ) and  ​ ω ^ α = ω ^ α 1 ​ … ​ ω ^ α k A_{i,mn}^{jk}(\omega)=\sum_{|\alpha|=m,|\beta|=n}{a_{i,\alpha\beta}^{jk}\hat{\omega}^{\alpha}\hat{\omega}^{\beta}},\qquad\text{where }\hat{\omega}=(-1,\omega)\quad\text{and }\hat{\omega}^{\alpha}=\hat{\omega}_{\alpha_{1}}...\hat{\omega}_{\alpha_{k}} 
 
 
 In fact, □ u = − ε − 1 ∂ s ∂ q ( r u ) + angular derivatives \square u=-\varepsilon^{-1}\partial_{s}\partial_{q}(ru)+\text{angular derivatives} and ∂ μ = ω ^ μ ∂ q + tangential derivatives \partial_{\mu}=\hat{\omega}_{\mu}\partial_{q}+\text{tangential derivatives} . 
 
 
 One can show that ( 6.1 )-( 6.3 ) has a solution
as long as ε ​ log ⁡ t \varepsilon\log{t} is bounded, provided that
 ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 is sufficiently small and the solution of
( 6.5 ) exists up to that time,
 [ J-K , H1 , H2 , L1 , L2 ] . The only exception is the case
 A i ​ 00 j ​ k ≠ 0 A^{jk}_{i00}\neq 0 , which has shorter life span. In cases where
the solution of ( 6.5 ) blows up it has been shown
that solutions of ( 6.1 )-( 6.3 ) also break
down in some finite time T ε ≤ e C / ε T_{\varepsilon}\leq e^{C/\varepsilon} ,
 [ J1 , H1 , A1 ] . John’s example was 
 

 
 (6.7) 
 
 □ ​ u = u t ​ △ ​ u \square u=u_{t}\,\triangle u 
 
 
 for which ( 6.5 ) is
the Burger’s equation
 ( 2 ∂ s − U q ∂ q ) U q = 0 (2\partial_{s}-U_{\!q}\partial_{q})U_{\!q}=0 , which is known to blow up.
The equation 
 

 
 (6.8) 
 
 □ ​ u = u t 2 \square u=u_{t}^{2} 
 
 
 is another example where solutions blow up,
for which ( 6.5 ) is ∂ s U q = U q 2 \partial_{s}U_{\!q}=U_{\!q}^{2} ,
that also blows up. 
 
 
 The null condition of [ K2 ] is equivalent to 
 

 
 (6.9) 
 
 A i ​ m ​ n j ​ k ​ ( ω ) = 0 for all  ​ ( i , j , k , m , n ) , ω ∈ 𝕊 2 . A_{i\,mn}^{jk}(\omega)=0\quad\text{for all }(i,j,k,m,n),\,\,\omega\in{\mathbb{S}}^{2}. 
 
 
 The results of [ C1 ] , [ K2 ] assert
that ( 6.1 )-( 6.3 ) has global solutions
for all sufficiently small initial data, provided that the null
condition is satisfies.
In this case the asymptotic equation
( 6.5 ) trivially can be solved globally.
Moreover, similar to the linear case, its solutions approach a
limit as s → ∞ s\!\to\!\infty 
and the solutions of ( 6.1 )-( 6.3 )
decay like solutions of linear equations. A typical example of an
equation satisfying the null condition is 
 

 
 (6.10) 
 
 □ ​ u = u t 2 − | ∇ x u | 2 \square u=u_{t}^{2}-|\nabla_{x}u|^{2} 
 
 
 There is however a more general class of nonlinearities for which solutions of
( 6.5 ) do not
blow up: 
 
 
 We say that
a system ( 6.1 ) satisfies the
 weak null condition if the solutions of the corresponding asymptotic
system
( 6.5 ) exist for all s s and if the solutions together
with its derivatives
grow at most exponentially in s s for all initial data decaying
sufficiently fast in q q . 
 
 
 Under the weak null condition assumption solutions of
( 6.5 )
satisfy the equation ( 6.1 ) up to terms of order
 ε 2 / | x | 3 − C ​ ε \varepsilon^{2}\!/|x|^{3-C\varepsilon}\!\! , but
need only decay like ε / | x | 1 − C ​ ε \varepsilon/|x|^{1-C\varepsilon}\!\! .
An example of the equation satisfying the weak null condition is given by 
 

 
 (6.11) 
 
 □ ​ u = u ​ △ ​ u \square u=u\triangle u 
 
 
 In [ L2 ] it was proven that ( 6.11 ) have small
global solutions in the spherically symmetric case and recently [ A3 ] 
established this result without the symmetry assumption. The
equation ( 6.11 )
appears to be similar to ( 6.7 ) but a closer look
shows that the corresponding asymptotic equation: 
 

 
 (6.12) 
 
 ( 2 ∂ s − U ∂ q ) U q = 0 (2\partial_{s}-U\partial_{q})U_{q}=0 
 
 
 has global solutions growing exponentially in s s , see [ L2 ] .
The system 
 

 
 (6.13) 
 
 □ ​ u = v t 2 , □ ​ v = 0 \square u=v_{t}^{2},\qquad\square v=0 
 
 
 is another example that satisfies the weak null condition.
The equation ( 6.13 ) appears to resemble ( 6.8 ).
The system however decouples:   v v satisfies a
linear homogeneous equation
and given v v we have a linear inhomogeneous equation for u u , and
global existence follows.
The corresponding asymptotic system is 
 

 
 (6.14) 
 
 ∂ s ∂ q U = ( ∂ q V ) 2 , ∂ s ∂ q V = 0 \partial_{s}\partial_{q}U=(\partial_{q}V)^{2},\qquad\partial_{s}\partial_{q}V=0 
 
 
 The solution of the second equation in ( 6.14 ) is
independent of s s : V q = V q ​ ( q , ω ) V_{q}=V_{q}(q,\omega) and substituting this into the first
equation we see that
 U q ​ ( s , q , ω ) = s ​ V q ​ ( q , ω ) 2 U_{q}(s,q,\omega)=sV_{q}(q,\omega)^{2} so ∂ u \partial u 
only decays like | x | − 1 ​ ln ⁡ | x | |x|^{-1}\ln{|x|} . 
 
 
 We show below that the Einstein vacuum equations in wave coordinates
satisfy the weak null condition, i.e.
that the corresponding
asymptotic system ( 6.5 )
admits global solutions. In fact, each of the quadratic nonlinear terms in the Einstein
equations is
either of the type appearing in
( 6.10 ), ( 6.11 ) or ( 6.13 ). 
 
 
 Theorem 6.1 . 
 
 Let h h be a symmetric 2-tensor and let 
 

 
 (6.15) 
 
 h μ ​ ν ​ ( t , x ) ∼ ε ​ U μ ​ ν ​ ( s , q , ω ) / | x | ,  where  q = | x | − t , s = ε ​ ln ⁡ | x | , ω = x / | x | . h_{\mu\nu}(t,x)\sim\varepsilon U_{\mu\nu}(s,q,\omega)/|x|,\quad\text{ where }\quad q=|x|-t,\,\,\,s=\varepsilon\ln{|x|},\,\,\,\omega=x/|x|. 
 
 
 be an asymptotic ansatz. Then the asymptotic
system for the the Einstein equations in wave coordinates
( 3.18 ), obtained by formally equating the terms with the
coefficients ϵ 2 ​ | x | − 2 \epsilon^{2}{|x|^{-2}} , takes the following form: 
 

 
 (6.16) 
 
 ( 2 ∂ s − U L ​ L ∂ q ) ∂ q U μ ​ ν = L μ L ν P ( ∂ q U , ∂ q U ) , ∀ μ , ν = 0 , … , 3 \big(2\partial_{s}-U_{LL}\partial_{q}\big)\partial_{q}U_{\mu\nu}=L_{\mu}L_{\nu}P(\partial_{q}U,\partial_{q}U),\qquad\forall\mu,\nu=0,...,3 
 
 
 where U L ​ L = m α ​ α ′ ​ m β ​ β ′ ​ U α ′ ​ β ′ ​ L α ​ L β U_{LL}=m^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}m^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}U_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}L_{\alpha}L_{\beta} and P ⁡ ( ∂ q U , ∂ q U ) = 1 4 ​ ∂ q tr ​ U ​ ∂ q tr ​ U − 1 2 ​ ∂ q U α ​ β ​ ∂ q U α ​ β P(\partial_{q}U,\partial_{q}U)=\frac{1}{4}\partial_{q}\text{tr}U\,\partial_{q}\text{tr}U-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q}U_{\alpha\beta}\,\partial_{q}U^{\alpha\beta} .
The asymptotic form of the wave coordinate condition
( 3.22 ) is 
 

 
 (6.17) 
 
 2 ∂ q U L ​ μ = L μ ∂ q tr U , ∀ μ = 0 , … , 3 , 2\partial_{q}U_{L\mu}=L_{\mu}\,\partial_{q}\operatorname{tr}U,\qquad\forall\mu=0,...,3, 
 
 
 where U L ​ μ = m α ​ α ′ ​ U α ′ ​ μ ​ L α U_{L\mu}=m^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}U_{\alpha^{\prime}\mu}L_{\alpha} and
 tr ​ U = m α ​ β ​ U α ​ β \text{tr}U=m^{\alpha\beta}U_{\alpha\beta} 
The solution of the system
( 6.16 )-( 6.17 ) exists globally and,
thus, the Einstein vacuum equations ( 3.18 ) in wave
coordinates satisfies the weak null condition. Moreover, the
component ∂ q U L ¯ ​ L ¯ \partial_{q}U_{\underline{L}\underline{L}} grows at most as s s while the
remaining components are uniformly bounded. 
 
 
 
 The asymptotic form ( 6.16 ) follows by a direct
calculation from ( 3.18 ).
Observe that the null form
 Q μ ​ ν ​ ( ∂ h , ∂ h ) Q_{\mu\nu}(\partial h,\partial h) 
disappears after passage to the asymptotic system. 
 
 
 Next we note that ( 6.17 ) is preserved under the flow of
( 6.16 ).

 (6.18)

( 6.16 ) with the vector field L ¯ \underline{L} . 
 

 
 (6.19) 
 
 ( 2 ∂ s − U L ​ L ∂ q ) ∂ q U L ¯ ​ L ¯ = 4 P ( ∂ q U , ∂ q U ) , (2\partial_{s}-U_{LL}\partial_{q})\partial_{q}U_{\underline{L}\underline{L}}=4P(\partial_{q}U,\partial_{q}U), 
 
 
 By Lemma 5.3 the quantity P ⁡ ( ∂ q U , ∂ q U ) P(\partial_{q}U,\partial_{q}U) does not contain the term ( ∂ q U L ¯ ​ L ¯ ) 2 (\partial_{q}U_{\underline{L}\underline{L}})^{2} .
Thus, the equation ( 6.19 ) can be solved globally and

with
the null frame

Therefore,

 7 Vector fields and commutators 

 
 Let Z ∈ 𝒵 Z\in{\cal Z} be any of the vector fields 
 

 
 
 Ω α ​ β = − x α ∂ β + x β ∂ α , S = t ∂ t + r ∂ r , ∂ α , \Omega_{\alpha\beta}=-x_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}+x_{\beta}\partial_{\alpha},\qquad S=t\partial_{t}+r\partial_{r},\qquad\partial_{\alpha}, 
 
 
 where x 0 = − t x_{0}=-t and x i = x i x_{i}=x^{i} , for i ≥ 1 i\geq 1 .
Let I = ( ι 1 , … , ι k ) I=(\iota_{1},...,\iota_{k}) , where | ι i | = 1 |\iota_{i}|=1 , be an ordered
multiindex of length | I | = k |I|=k and let Z I = Z ι 1 ⋯ Z ι k Z^{I}=Z^{\iota_{1}}\cdot\cdot\cdot Z^{\iota_{k}} denote a product of | I | |I| 
such derivatives. With a slight abuse of notation we will also identify
the index set with vector fields, so I = Z I=Z means the index I I corresponding to
the vector field Z Z . Furthermore, by a sum over I 1 + I 2 = I I_{1}+I_{2}=I we mean a sum
over all possible order preserving
partitions of the ordered multiindex I I into two
ordered multiindices I 1 I_{1} and I 2 I_{2} , i.e.
if I = ( ι 1 , … , ι k ) I=(\iota_{1},...,\iota_{k}) , then
 I 1 = ( ι i 1 , … , ι i n ) I_{1}=(\iota_{i_{1}},...,\iota_{i_{n}}) 
and I 2 = ( ι i n + 1 , … , ι i k ) I_{2}=(\iota_{i_{n+1}},...,\iota_{i_{k}}) ,
where i 1 , … , i k i_{1},...,i_{k} is any reordering of the integers 1 , … , k 1,...,k such that i 1 < … < i n i_{1}<...<i_{n} and i n + 1 < … < i k i_{n+1}<...<i_{k} 
and i 1 , … , i k i_{1},...,i_{k} .
With this convention
Leibniz rule becomes Z I ​ ( f ​ g ) = ∑ I 1 + I 2 = I ( Z I 1 ​ f ) ​ ( Z I 2 ​ g ) Z^{I}(fg)=\sum_{I_{1}+I_{2}=I}(Z^{I_{1}}f)(Z^{I_{2}}g) . We denote by ∂ ¯ \bar{\partial} the tangential derivatives, i.e.,
 ∂ ¯ = { ∂ ¯ 0 , ∂ ¯ 1 , ∂ ¯ 2 , ∂ ¯ 3 } \bar{\partial}=\{\bar{\partial}_{0},\bar{\partial}_{1},\bar{\partial}_{2},\bar{\partial}_{3}\} and note
that the span of the tangential derivatives { ∂ ¯ 0 , ∂ ¯ 1 , ∂ ¯ 2 , ∂ ¯ 3 } \{\bar{\partial}_{0},\bar{\partial}_{1},\bar{\partial}_{2},\bar{\partial}_{3}\} coincides with the
linear span of the vectorfields { ∂ s , ∂ S 1 , ∂ S 2 } \{\partial_{s},\partial_{S_{1}},\partial_{S_{2}}\} . 
 
 
 Lemma 7.1 . 
 
 We have the following expressions for the coordinate vector
fields: 
 

 
 (7.1) 
 
 
 ∂ t = t ​ S − x i ​ Ω 0 ​ i t 2 − r 2 , \displaystyle\partial_{t}=\frac{tS-x^{i}\Omega_{0i}}{t^{2}-r^{2}}, 
 
 
 (7.2) 
 
 
 ∂ r = ω i ∂ i = t ​ ω i ​ Ω 0 ​ i − r ​ S t 2 − r 2 , \displaystyle\partial_{r}=\omega^{i}\partial_{i}=\frac{t\omega^{i}\Omega_{0i}-rS}{t^{2}-r^{2}}, 
 
 
 (7.3) 
 
 
 ∂ i = − x j ​ Ω i ​ j + t ​ Ω 0 ​ i − x i ​ S t 2 − r 2 = − x i ​ S t 2 − r 2 + x i ​ x j ​ Ω 0 ​ j t ⁡ ( t 2 − r 2 ) + Ω 0 ​ i t \displaystyle\partial_{i}=\frac{-x^{j}\Omega_{ij}+t\Omega_{0i}-x_{i}S}{t^{2}-r^{2}}=-\frac{x_{i}S}{t^{2}-r^{2}}+\frac{x_{i}x^{j}\Omega_{0j}}{t(t^{2}-r^{2})}+\frac{\Omega_{0i}}{t} 
 
 
 In particular, 
 

 
 (7.4) 
 
 ∂ s = 1 2 ( ∂ t + ∂ r ) = S + ω i ​ Ω 0 ​ i 2 ​ ( t + r ) , ∂ ¯ i = ∂ i − ω i ∂ r = ω j ​ Ω i ​ j r = − ω i ​ ω j ​ Ω 0 ​ j + Ω 0 ​ i t . \partial_{s}=\frac{1}{2}\big(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r}\big)=\frac{S+\omega^{i}\Omega_{0i}}{2(t+r)},\qquad\bar{\partial}_{i}=\partial_{i}-\omega_{i}\partial_{r}=\frac{\omega^{j}\Omega_{ij}}{r}=\frac{-\omega_{i}\omega^{j}\Omega_{0j}+\Omega_{0i}}{t}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Lemma 7.2 . 
 
 For any function f f we have the estimate 
 

 
 (7.5) 
 
 ( 1 + t + | q | ) ​ | ∂ ¯ ​ f | + ( 1 + | q | ) ​ | ∂ f | ≲ C ​ ∑ | I | = 1 | Z I ​ f | , | ∂ f | ≲ | ∂ ¯ ​ f | + | ∂ q f | (1+t+|q|)|\bar{\partial}f|+(1+|q|)|\partial f|\lesssim C\sum_{|I|=1}|Z^{I}f|,\qquad\quad|\partial f|\lesssim|\bar{\partial}f|+|\partial_{q}f| 
 
 
 where | ∂ ¯ ​ f | 2 = | ∂ ¯ 0 ​ f | 2 + | ∂ ¯ 1 ​ f | 2 + | ∂ ¯ 2 ​ f | 2 + | ∂ ¯ 3 ​ f | 2 |\bar{\partial}f|^{2}=|\bar{\partial}_{0}f|^{2}+|\bar{\partial}_{1}f|^{2}+|\bar{\partial}_{2}f|^{2}+|\bar{\partial}_{3}f|^{2} and ∂ ¯ 0 = ∂ s \bar{\partial}_{0}=\partial_{s} . Furthermore 
 

 
 (7.6) 
 
 | ∂ ¯ 2 ​ f | ≲ C r ​ ∑ | I | ≤ 2 | Z I ​ f | 1 + t + | q | , |\bar{\partial}^{2}f|\lesssim\frac{C}{r}\sum_{|I|\leq 2}\frac{|Z^{I}f|}{1+t+|q|}, 
 
 
 where | ∂ ¯ 2 ​ f | 2 = ∑ α , β = 0 , 1 , 2 , 3 | ∂ ¯ α ​ ∂ ¯ β ​ f | 2 |\bar{\partial}^{2}f|^{2}=\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0,1,2,3}|\bar{\partial}_{\alpha}\bar{\partial}_{\beta}f|^{2} . 
 
 
 Moreover, if k α ​ β k^{\alpha\beta} is a symmetric tensor then 
 

 
 (7.7) 
 
 | k α ​ β ​ ∂ α ∂ β ϕ | ≤ C ⁡ ( | k | 1 + t + | q | + | k | L ​ L 1 + | q | ) ​ ∑ | I | ≤ 1 | ∂ Z I ​ ϕ | |k^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\phi|\leq C\bigg(\frac{|k|}{1+t+|q|}+\frac{|k|_{LL}}{1+|q|}\bigg)\sum_{|I|\leq 1}|\partial Z^{I}\phi| 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 First we note that if r + t ≤ 1 r+t\leq 1 then
( 7.5 ) holds since the usual derivatives ∂ α \partial_{\alpha} are

usual derivatives. Since | Ω i ​ j ​ ω k | ≤ C |\Omega_{ij}\omega_{k}|\leq C and

by applying ∂ ¯ i = r − 1 ​ ω j ​ Ω i ​ j \bar{\partial}_{i}=r^{-1}\omega^{j}\Omega_{ij} to the

 | ∂ ¯ i ​ ∂ ¯ α ​ f | ≤ C ​ r − 1 ​ ( t + r ) − 1 ​ ∑ | I | ≤ 2 | Z I ​ f | . |\bar{\partial}_{i}\bar{\partial}_{\alpha}f|\leq Cr^{-1}(t+r)^{-1}\sum_{|I|\leq 2}|Z^{I}f|.

( 7.6 ) for ∂ s 2 \partial_{s}^{2} . Since S ​ ω j = 0 S\omega^{j}=0 ,

for ∂ s 2 \partial_{s}^{2} . 
 
 
 The inequality ( 7.7 ) follows from

 Lemma 7.3 .

 (7.9) 
 
 | ( 4 ∂ s − H L ​ L 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ ∂ q − tr ¯ ​ H + H L ​ L ¯ 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ ​ r ) ∂ q ( r ϕ ) + r ​ F 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ | ≲ ( 1 + r ​ | H | ℒ𝒯 1 + | q | + | H | ) r − 1 ∑ | I | ≤ 2 | Z I ϕ | \Big|\Big(4\partial_{s}-\frac{H_{LL}}{2g^{L\underline{L}}}\partial_{q}-\frac{\overline{\operatorname{tr}}\,H\,+H_{L\underline{L}}}{2g^{L\underline{L}}\,\,r}\Big)\partial_{q}(r\phi)+\frac{rF}{2g^{L\underline{L}}}\Big|\lesssim\Big(1+\frac{r\,|H|_{\cal LT}}{1+|q|}+|H|\Big)r^{-1}\sum_{|I|\leq 2}|Z^{I}\phi|

 | ( 4 ∂ s − H L ​ L 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ ∂ q − tr ¯ ​ H + H L ​ L ¯ 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ ​ r ) ∂ q ( r ϕ ) + r ​ F 2 ​ g L ​ L ¯ | ≲ r | △ ω ​ ϕ ​ | + r ​ | H | L ​ 𝒯 ​ | ∂ ¯ ​ ∂ ϕ | + | ​ H | ​ ( r ​ | ∂ ¯ 2 ​ ϕ | + | ∂ ¯ ​ ϕ | + r − 1 ​ | ϕ | ) \Big|\Big(4\partial_{s}-\frac{H_{LL}}{2g^{L\underline{L}}}\partial_{q}-\frac{\overline{\operatorname{tr}}\,H\,+H_{L\underline{L}}}{2g^{L\underline{L}}\,\,r}\Big)\partial_{q}(r\phi)+\frac{rF}{2g^{L\underline{L}}}\Big|\\

 Lemma 7.4 . 
 
 Let Z = Z μ ∂ μ Z=Z^{\mu}\partial_{\mu} be any of the vector fields above and let c α μ c_{\alpha}^{\mu} 
be defined by

 where c Z c_{Z} is either 0 0 or 2 2 .

 (7.10) 
 
 Z ​ Q ​ ( ∂ ϕ , ∂ ψ ) = Q ⁡ ( ∂ ϕ , ∂ Z ​ ψ ) + Q ⁡ ( ∂ Z ​ ϕ , ∂ ψ ) + Q ~ ​ ( ∂ ϕ , ∂ ψ ) ZQ(\partial\phi,\partial\psi)=Q(\partial\phi,\partial Z\psi)+Q(\partial Z\phi,\partial\psi)+\tilde{Q}(\partial\phi,\partial\psi) 
 
 
 for some null form Q ~ \tilde{Q} on the right hand-side. 
 
 
 
 Proof.

In particular, 
 

 
 
 L α ​ L β ​ ∂ α Z β = 0 . L^{\alpha}L^{\beta}\partial_{\alpha}Z_{\beta}=0. 
 
 
 If c α μ c_{\alpha}^{\,\,\,\mu} is as defined above and

the above simply means that

 Lemma 7.5 . 
 
 If k α ​ β k^{\alpha\beta} is a symmetric tensor then 
 

 
 (7.12)

 | k Z | L ​ L ≤ 2 ​ | k | L ​ 𝒯 . |k_{Z}|_{LL}\leq 2|k|_{L\cal T}.

 where 
 

 
 (7.15) 
 
 k J ​ α ​ β = ∑ | K | ≤ | J | c K ​ μ ​ ν J ​ α ​ β ​ Z K ​ k μ ​ ν = − Z J ​ k α ​ β − ∑ K + Z = J Z K ​ k Z α ​ β + ∑ | K | ≤ | J | − 2 d K ​ μ ​ ν J ​ α ​ β ​ Z K ​ k μ ​ ν k^{J\alpha\beta}=\!\sum_{|K|\leq|J|}c^{J\alpha\beta}_{K\mu\nu}Z^{K}k^{\mu\nu}=-Z^{J}k^{\alpha\beta}-\!\sum_{K+Z=J}Z^{K}k_{Z}^{\alpha\beta}+\!\!\sum_{|K|\leq|J|-2}d^{J\alpha\beta}_{K\mu\nu}Z^{K}k^{\mu\nu} 
 
 
 for some constants

into two ordered multiindices I 1 I_{1} and I 2 I_{2} . 
 
 
 
 Proof.

and x x we have

 which proves the first statement and the second follows since

 (7.19) 
 
 □ ~ g Z I ϕ − Z ^ I □ ~ g ϕ = − ∑ I 1 + I 2 = I , | I 2 | < | I | H ^ I 1 ​ α ​ β ∂ α ∂ β Z I 2 ϕ , \widetilde{\square}_{g}Z^{I}\phi-\hat{Z}^{I}\widetilde{\square}_{g}\phi=-\sum_{I_{1}+I_{2}=I,\,|I_{2}|<|I|}\hat{H}^{{I}_{1}\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}Z^{I_{2}}\phi, 
 
 
 where

 H ^ J ​ α ​ β = ∑ | M | ≤ | J | c M ​ μ ​ ν J ​ α ​ β ​ Z ^ M ​ H μ ​ ν = − Z ^ J ​ H α ​ β − ∑ M + Z = J Z ^ M ​ H Z α ​ β + ∑ | M | ≤ | J | − 2 d M ​ μ ​ ν J ​ α ​ β ​ Z ^ M ​ H μ ​ ν \hat{H}^{J\alpha\beta}=\!\sum_{|M|\leq|J|}{c}^{J\alpha\beta}_{M\mu\nu}\hat{Z}^{M}H^{\mu\nu}=-\hat{Z}^{J}H^{\alpha\beta}-\!\sum_{M+Z=J}\hat{Z}^{M}H_{Z}^{\alpha\beta}+\!\!\sum_{|M|\leq|J|-2}{d}^{J\alpha\beta}_{M\mu\nu}\hat{Z}^{M}H^{\mu\nu}

 (7.21) 
 
 | □ ~ g ​ Z I ​ ϕ | ≲ | Z ^ I ​ □ ~ g ​ ϕ | + 1 1 + t + | q | ​ ∑ | K | ≤ | I | , ∑ | J | + ( | K | − 1 ) + ≤ | I | | Z J ​ H | ​ | ∂ Z K ​ ϕ | \displaystyle|\widetilde{\square}_{g}Z^{I}\phi|\lesssim|\hat{Z}^{I}\widetilde{\square}_{g}\phi|+\frac{1}{1+t+|q|}\,\,\,\sum_{|K|\leq|I|,}\,\,\sum_{|J|+(|K|-1)_{+}\leq|I|}\,\,\,|Z^{J}H|\,\,{|\partial Z^{K}\phi|} 
 
 
 
 + 1 1 + | q | ∑ | K | ≤ | I | ( ∑ | J | + ( | K | − 1 ) + ≤ | I | | Z J H | L ​ L + ∑ | J ′ | + ( | K | − 1 ) + ≤ | I | − 1 | Z J ′ H | L ​ 𝒯 + ∑ | J ′′ | + ( | K | − 1 ) + ≤ | I | − 2 | Z J ′′ H | ) | ∂ Z K ϕ | \displaystyle+\frac{1}{1+|q|}\sum_{|K|\leq|I|}\Big(\sum_{|J|+(|K|-1)_{+}\leq|I|}\!\!\!\!\!|Z^{J}H|_{LL}+\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{|J^{\prime}|+(|K|-1)_{+}\leq|I|-1}\!\!\!\!\!|Z^{J^{\prime}}H|_{L\cal T}+\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{|J^{\prime\prime}|+(|K|-1)_{+}\leq|I|-2}\!\!\!\!\!|Z^{J^{\prime\prime}}H|\Big){|\partial Z^{K}\phi|} 
 
 
 where ( | K | − 1 ) + = | K | − 1 (|K|-1)_{+}=|K|-1 if | K | ≥ 1 |K|\geq 1 and ( | K | − 1 ) + = 0 (|K|-1)_{+}=0 if | K | = 0 |K|=0 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 First observe that 
 

 
 
 Z ^ ​ □ ~ g ​ ϕ \displaystyle\hat{Z}\widetilde{\square}_{g}\phi

 The inequality ( 7.18 ) now follows from
( 7.17 ), ( 7.13 )
and the estimate ( 7.7 ).

follows from the following
calculation, similar to the one in Lemma 7.5 .
We have 
 

 
 
 Z ^ I ​ □ ~ g ​ ϕ = Z ^ I ​ □ ​ ϕ + Z ^ I ​ H α ​ β ​ ∂ α ​ β 2 ϕ = □ ​ Z I ​ ϕ + ∑ J + K = I Z ^ J ​ H α ​ β ​ Z K ​ ∂ α ​ β 2 ϕ \hat{Z}^{I}\widetilde{\square}_{g}\phi=\hat{Z}^{I}\square\phi+\hat{Z}^{I}H^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}\phi=\square Z^{I}\phi+\sum_{J+K=I}\hat{Z}^{J}H^{\alpha\beta}Z^{K}\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}\phi 
 
 
 If we now use ( 7.16 ) we get
( 7.19 ) as in the proof of
Lemma 7.5 . The inequality ( 7.21 ) now follows from
( 7.19 ), ( 7.13 )
and the estimate ( 7.7 ).

 8 Basic energy identities 

 
 We now establish basic energy identities for solutions of the
equation 
 

 
 (8.1) 
 
 □ ~ g ​ ϕ = F \widetilde{\square}_{g}\phi=F 
 
 
 We denote by
 Σ t \Sigma_{t} the hyper surfaces t = t= const, by C t 1 t 2 ​ ( q ) C_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}(q) 
the forward light cones with a vertex at ( q , 0 ) (q,0) and truncated at
times t 1 , t 2 t_{1},t_{2} . We also denote by K t 1 t 2 ​ ( q ) K_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}(q) the
interior of the light cone C t 1 t 2 ​ ( q ) C_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}(q) and by B t , r B_{t,r} 
the ball of radius r r centered at ( t , 0 ) (t,0) . 
 
 
 Lemma 8.1 . 
 
 Let ϕ \phi be a solution of ( 8.1 ). Then for
any t 1 ≤ t 2 t_{1}\leq t_{2} and an arbitrary q ≤ t 2 q\leq t_{2} 
 

 
 
 ∫ Σ t 2 ( − g 00 ​ | ∂ t ϕ | 2 + g i ​ j ​ ∂ i ϕ ​ ∂ j ϕ ) \displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{t_{2}}}\big(-g^{00}|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}+g^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi\big) 
 = ∫ Σ t 1 ( − g 00 ​ | ∂ t ϕ | 2 + g i ​ j ​ ∂ i ϕ ​ ∂ j ϕ ) \displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{t_{1}}}\big(-g^{00}|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}+g^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi\big) 
 
 
 (8.2) 
 
 
 − 2 ∫ t 1 t 2 ∫ Σ τ ( ∂ α g α ​ β ∂ β ϕ ∂ t ϕ − 1 2 ∂ t g α ​ β ∂ α ϕ ∂ β ϕ + F ∂ t ϕ ) , \displaystyle-2\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\Big(\partial_{\alpha}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi\partial_{t}\phi-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial_{\beta}\phi+F\partial_{t}\phi\Big), 
 
 
 and 
 

 
 
 ∫ B t 1 − q ( − g 00 ​ | ∂ t ϕ | 2 + g i ​ j ​ ∂ i ϕ ​ ∂ j ϕ ) \displaystyle\int_{B_{t_{1}-q}}\big(-g^{00}|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}+g^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi\big) 
 + ∫ C t 1 t 2 ​ ( q ) | ∂ ¯ ϕ | 2 = ∫ B t 2 − q ( − g 00 | ∂ t ϕ | 2 + g i ​ j ∂ i ϕ ∂ j ϕ ) \displaystyle+\int_{C_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}(q)}|\bar{\partial}\phi|^{2}=\int_{B_{t_{2}-q}}\big(-g^{00}|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}+g^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi\big) 
 
 
 
 
 + 2 ∫ K t 1 t 2 ​ ( q ) ( ∂ α g α ​ β ∂ β ϕ ∂ t ϕ − 1 2 ∂ t g α ​ β ∂ α ϕ ∂ β ϕ + F ∂ t ϕ ) \displaystyle+2\int_{K_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}(q)}\Big(\partial_{\alpha}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi\partial_{t}\phi-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial_{\beta}\phi+F\partial_{t}\phi\Big) 
 
 
 (8.3) 
 
 
 + 2 ∫ C t 1 t 2 ​ ( q ) ( 2 ( g α ​ β − m α ​ β ) L α ∂ β ϕ ∂ t ϕ + ( g α ​ β − m α ​ β ) ∂ α ϕ ∂ β ϕ ) \displaystyle+2\int_{C_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}(q)}\big(2(g^{\alpha\beta}-m^{\alpha\beta})L_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\phi\partial_{t}\phi+(g^{\alpha\beta}-m^{\alpha\beta})\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial_{\beta}\phi\big) 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We multiply the equation ( 8.1 ) by ∂ t ϕ \partial_{t}\phi 
and integrate over the space-time slab between the hyper surfaces
 Σ t 1 \Sigma_{t_{1}} and Σ t 2 \Sigma_{t_{2}} . We have 
 

 
 
 − ∫ t 1 t 2 ∫ Σ τ g α ​ β ∂ 2 α ​ β ϕ ∂ t ϕ \displaystyle-\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}\phi\partial_{t}\phi 
 = ∫ t 1 t 2 ∫ Σ τ ( g α ​ β ​ ∂ β ϕ ​ ∂ t ∂ α ϕ + ∂ α g α ​ β ​ ∂ β ϕ ​ ∂ t ϕ ) \displaystyle=\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\big(g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi\partial_{t}\partial_{\alpha}\phi+\partial_{\alpha}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi\partial_{t}\phi\big) 
 
 
 
 
 − ∫ Σ t 2 g 0 ​ β ∂ β ϕ ∂ t ϕ + ∫ Σ t 1 g 0 ​ β ∂ β ϕ ∂ t ϕ \displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma_{t_{2}}}g^{0\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi\partial_{t}\phi+\int_{\Sigma_{t_{1}}}g^{0\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi\partial_{t}\phi 
 
 
 
 
 = 1 2 ​ ∫ Σ t 2 ( − g 00 ​ | ∂ t ϕ | 2 + g i ​ j ​ ∂ i ϕ ​ ∂ j ϕ ) − 1 2 ​ ∫ Σ t 1 ( − g 00 ​ | ∂ t ϕ | 2 + g i ​ j ​ ∂ i ϕ ​ ∂ j ϕ ) \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma_{t_{2}}}\big(-g^{00}|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}+g^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi\big)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma_{t_{1}}}\big(-g^{00}|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}+g^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi\big) 
 
 
 
 
 + ∫ t 1 t 2 ∫ Σ τ ( ∂ α g α ​ β ∂ β ϕ ∂ t ϕ − 1 2 ∂ t g α ​ β ∂ α ϕ ∂ β ϕ ) \displaystyle+\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\big(\partial_{\alpha}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi\partial_{t}\phi-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial_{\beta}\phi\big) 
 
 
 and the desired identity ( 8.2 ) follows.
Similarly, integrating over the region K t 1 t 2 ​ ( q ) K_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}(q) we obtain 
 

 
 
 ∫ B t 1 − q ( − g 00 ​ | ∂ t ϕ | 2 + g i ​ j ​ ∂ i ϕ ​ ∂ j ϕ ) \displaystyle\int_{B_{t_{1}-q}}\big(-g^{00}|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}+g^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi\big)

 = ∫ B t 2 − q ( − g 00 ​ | ∂ t ϕ | 2 + g i ​ j ​ ∂ i ϕ ​ ∂ j ϕ ) \displaystyle=\int_{B_{t_{2}-q}}\big(-g^{00}|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}+g^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi\big) 
 
 
 
 
 + 2 ∫ K t 1 t 2 ​ ( q ) ( ∂ α g α ​ β ∂ β ϕ ∂ t ϕ − 1 2 ∂ t g α ​ β ∂ α ϕ ∂ β ϕ + F ∂ t ϕ ) \displaystyle+2\int_{K_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}(q)}\Big(\partial_{\alpha}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi\partial_{t}\phi-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial_{\beta}\phi+F\partial_{t}\phi\Big) 
 
 
 Subtracting the Minkowski part from the metric g g in the
 C t 1 t 2 ​ ( q ) C_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}(q) integral leads to the identity
( 8.3 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary 8.2 . 
 
 Let ϕ \phi be a solution of the equation ( 8.1 )
with a metric g g satisfying the condition that 
 

 
 (8.4) 
 
 | H | ≤ 1 4 , H α ​ β = g α ​ β − m α ​ β . |H|\leq\frac{1}{4},\qquad H^{\alpha\beta}=g^{\alpha\beta}-m^{\alpha\beta}. 
 
 
 Then for any 0 < γ ≤ 1 0<\gamma\leq 1 
 

 
 
 ∫ Σ t 2 ( | ∂ t ϕ | 2 + | ∇ x ϕ | 2 ) \displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{t_{2}}}\big(|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}+|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}\big) 
 + ∫ t 1 t 2 ∫ Σ τ γ ​ | ∂ ¯ ​ ϕ | 2 ( 1 + | q | ) 1 + 2 ​ γ ≤ 4 ∫ Σ t 1 ( | ∂ t ϕ | 2 + | ∇ x ϕ | 2 ) \displaystyle+\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\frac{\gamma\,\,|\bar{\partial}\phi|^{2}}{(1+|q|)^{1+2\gamma}}\leq 4\int_{\Sigma_{t_{1}}}\big(|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}+|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}\big) 
 
 
 (8.5) 
 
 
 + 8 ∫ t 1 t 2 ∫ Σ τ | ∂ α g α ​ β ∂ β ϕ ∂ t ϕ − 1 2 ∂ t g α ​ β ∂ α ϕ ∂ β ϕ + F ∂ t ϕ | \displaystyle+8\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\big|\partial_{\alpha}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi\partial_{t}\phi-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial_{\beta}\phi+F\partial_{t}\phi\big| 
 
 
 
 
 + 2 ∫ t 1 t 2 ∫ Σ τ γ ( 1 + | q | ) 1 + 2 ​ γ | ( g α ​ β − m α ​ β ) ∂ α ϕ ∂ β ϕ + 2 ( g L ¯ ​ β − m L ¯ ​ β ) ∂ β ϕ ∂ t ϕ | \displaystyle+2\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\frac{\gamma}{(1+|q|)^{1+2\gamma}}\big|(g^{\alpha\beta}-m^{\alpha\beta})\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial_{\beta}\phi+2(g^{\underline{L}\beta}-m^{\underline{L}\beta})\partial_{\beta}\phi\partial_{t}\phi\big| 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 First we note that ( 8.4 ) implies that 
 

 
 (8.6) 
 
 3 4 ​ ( | ∂ t ϕ | 2 + | ∇ x ϕ | 2 ) ≤ − g 00 ​ | ∂ t ϕ | 2 + g i ​ j ​ ∂ i ϕ ​ ∂ j ϕ ≤ 5 4 ​ ( | ∂ t ϕ | 2 + | ∇ x ϕ | 2 ) \frac{3}{4}\big(|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}+|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}\big)\leq-g^{00}|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}+g^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\,\partial_{j}\phi\leq\frac{5}{4}\big(|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}+|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}\big)

 ≤ ∫ Σ t 1 ( − g 00 ​ | ∂ t ϕ | 2 + g i ​ j ​ ∂ i ϕ ​ ∂ j ϕ ) \displaystyle\leq\int_{\Sigma_{t_{1}}}\big(-g^{00}|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}+g^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi\big)

 We multiply the above inequality by an integrable factor

respect to q q in the interval ( − ∞ , t 2 ] (-\infty,t_{2}] to obtain: 
 

 
 
 ∫ t 1 t 2 ∫ Σ t γ ​ | ∂ ¯ ​ ϕ | 2 ( 1 + | q | ) 1 + 2 ​ γ \displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{t}}\frac{\gamma|\bar{\partial}\phi|^{2}}{(1+|q|)^{1+2\gamma}} 
 ≤ 5 4 ​ ∫ Σ t 1 ( | ∂ t ϕ | 2 + | ∇ x ϕ | 2 ) \displaystyle\leq\frac{5}{4}\int_{\Sigma_{t_{1}}}\big(|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}+|\nabla_{x}\phi|^{2}\big)

 and the corollary follows.
